High-Dimensional Tensor Discriminant Analysis: Low-Rank Discriminant Structure, Representation Synergy, and Theoretical Guarantees
Elynn Chen † Yuefeng Han ♯ Jiayu Li ♭ †,♭ New York University ♯ University of Notre Dame

Abstract
High-dimensional tensor-valued predictors arise in modern applications, increasingly as learned representations from neural networks. Existing tensor classification methods rely on sparsity or Tucker structures and often lack theoretical guarantees. Motivated by empirical evidence that discriminative signals concentrate along a few multilinear components, we introduce CP low-rank structure for the discriminant tensor, a modeling perspective not previously explored. Under a Tensor Gaussian Mixture Model, we propose high-dimensional CP low-rank Tensor Discriminant Analysis (CP-TDA) with Randomized Composite PCA ( rc-PCA ) initialization, that is essential for handling dependent and anisotropic noise under weaker signal strength and incoherence conditions, followed by iterative refinement algorithm. We establish global convergence and minimax-optimal misclassification rates. To handle tensor data deviating from tensor normality, we develop the first semiparametric tensor discriminant model, in which learned tensor representations are mapped via deep generative models into a latent space tailored for CP-TDA. Misclassification risk decomposes into representation, approximation, and estimation errors. Numerical studies and real data analysis on graph classification demonstrate substantial gains over existing tensor classifiers and state-of-the-art graph neural networks, particularly in high-dimensional, small-sample regimes.



Keywords:

Tensor classification;
Linear discriminant analysis;
Tensor iterative projection;
CP low-rank;
High-dimensional data;
Semiparametric tensor discriminant analysis;
Deep generative model.

1  Introduction

High-dimensional tensor-valued data pervade modern scientific applications, from neuroimaging
(
Zhou et al., 2013
)
and economics
(
Chen and Fan, 2021
;
Chen et al., 2020
;
Mo et al., 2025
)
to recommendation systems
(
Bi et al., 2018
)
and climate analysis
(
Chen et al., 2024b
)
. Beyond naturally occurring tensors, tensor predictors increasingly arise as learned representations from neural networks or outputs of multimodal integration pipelines
(
Hao et al., 2013
;
Huang et al., 2025
)
. These settings share two key challenges: (i) tensor dimensionality grows multiplicatively across modes, and (ii) discriminant structure governing class differences is often concentrated in a low-dimensional multilinear subspace
(
Wen et al., 2024
;
Wu et al., 2025
)
. Existing classification approaches, including sparse tensor linear discriminant analysis (LDA)
(
Pan et al., 2019
)
, tensor regressions
(
Zhou et al., 2013
;
Xia et al., 2022
)
, and deep neural networks
(
Chien and Bao, 2018
;
Jahromi and Orús, 2024
)
, address parts of this problem, but do not explicitly impose or exploit low-rank structure in the
discriminant tensor
itself, nor do they provide comprehensive high-dimensional theoretical guarantees for misclassification risk.

This paper develops a new
tensor discriminant analysis
(TDA) framework addressing both issues. Motivated by empirical evidence that discriminant signals in tensor predictors are often well-approximated by a few multilinear directions, especially for learned tensor features
(
Wen et al., 2024
;
Wu et al., 2025
)
, we introduce
CP low-rank discriminant tensors
. To the best of our knowledge, the low-rank structure of the discriminant tensor has not been systematically studied, despite the prevalence of low multilinear rank in tensor representations.
Under a Tensor Gaussian Mixture Model (TGMM) with common mode-wise covariances, we develop
the first tensor LDA with a CP low-rank discriminant tensor
(CP-TDA), which preserves multiway structure, reduces effective dimensionality, and yields interpretable mode-specific discriminant directions.
The discriminant tensor is estimated via a new Randomized Composite PCA (
rc-PCA
) initialization that lies within the basin of attraction of the global optimum, followed by iterative refinement.

Modern tensor data, however, especially those arising from deep learning representations or multimodal integration, rarely follow tensor normal distributions. To address this broader regime, we propose Semiparametric Tensor Discriminant Networks (STDN), a semiparametric extension combining tensor discriminant analysis with deep learning. Unlike existing semiparametric LDA based on restrictive elementwise monotone transformations, we introduce a tensor Gaussianizing flow that transforms tensor representations, from any neural network encoder, into a latent space that approximately satisfies a TGMM. In this space, CP-TDA is the Bayes-optimal classifier under the surrogate model. This offers a principled alternative to dense neural network classifier heads: it preserves the interpretability of discriminant analysis while leveraging the representational power of deep neural networks. To the best of our knowledge, this is
the first use of deep learning to construct semiparametric tensor discriminant analysis with flexible nonlinear transformations
.

Beyond methodological contributions, we also provide new theoretical guarantees for CP-TDA and its semiparametric extension. For CP-TDA, we develop perturbation analysis for
rc-PCA
under dependent, heteroskedastic noise induced by sample discriminant tensors, relaxing the stringent eigen-gap and incoherence conditions used in prior CP decomposition work
(
Anandkumar et al., 2014b
;
Sun et al., 2017
;
Han et al., 2024b
)
. This makes
rc-PCA
valuable beyond tensor LDA, including CP decomposition and regression where existing initializations fail. We establish non-asymptotic global convergence rates for the high-dimensional CP low-rank discriminant tensor and derive the first minimax-optimal misclassification rates for CP-TDA. For the semiparametric framework, we obtain excess misclassification risk bounds that separate three sources of error: CP low-rank approximation error, statistical error under CP-TDA, and Gaussianizing flow representation error, thereby providing guarantees absent in current deep-learning-based tensor classifiers.

Finally, we illustrate the practical benefits of our framework on two bioinformatics graph datasets, using deep learning encoders to produce tensor representations. The proposed semiparametric tensor LDA consistently outperforms both existing tensor classifiers on tensor features and state-of-the-art graph neural networks. These results underscore the central message of this work: carefully designed statistical structure, here, CP low-rank discriminant modeling under TGMM, can be effectively combined with modern deep learning to achieve both interpretability and strong empirical performance, not as a competitor to deep learning but as a statistically principled complement.

1.1
Related Work and Our Distinction

Tensor decomposition and regression.

Tensor methods have become essential for multiway data analysis, with advances in tensor decomposition
(
Sun et al., 2017
;
Zhang and Xia, 2018
;
Chen et al., 2025
)
, tensor regression
(
Zhou et al., 2013
;
Li and Zhang, 2017
;
Xia et al., 2022
;
Chen et al., 2024a
)
, and tensor clustering
(
Sun and Li, 2019
;
Han et al., 2022a
;
Luo and Zhang, 2022
;
Hu and Wang, 2023
)
. Most work exploits low-rank structure, with Tucker-type models widely used for parameter reduction
(
Xia et al., 2022
;
Han et al., 2022b
)
. In contrast, CP-based low-rank modeling is far less developed:
Zhou et al., 2013
employ a block relaxation algorithm for a highly non-convex CP tensor generalized linear model without principled initialization, leading to unreliable convergence in high dimensions (see Appendix
C
).

High-dimensional vector discriminant analysis.

Vector-based discriminant analysis extends Fisher’s LDA to high dimensions through sparse and direct LDA methods
(
Mai et al., 2012
;
Cai and Zhang, 2019
)
, feature screening for multiclass LDA
(
Pan et al., 2016
)
, and high-dimensional quadratic discriminant analysis
(
Cai and Zhang, 2021
)
. However, these approaches operate on vectorized data, losing multiway structure and mode-specific interactions inherent in tensor data.

Tensor classification.

Recent tensor classifiers include support tensor machines
(
Hao et al., 2013
;
Guo et al., 2016
)
, tensor logistic regression
(
Liu et al., 2020
;
Song et al., 2023
)
, and tensor neural networks
(
Chien and Bao, 2018
;
Jahromi and Orús, 2024
;
Wen et al., 2024
)
. Tensor multilinear discriminant analysis
(
Lai et al., 2013
;
Dufrenois et al., 2023
)
extracts multilinear features but relies on subsequent vector classifiers. While empirically effective, these methods typically lack theoretical guarantees and ignore mode-wise covariance structure. On the theoretical side,
Han et al., 2022b
analyze tensor logistic regression under i.i.d. entries for each tensor predictor, while
Pan et al., 2019
study tensor LDA with sparsity in a TGMM, and
Li et al., 2024
and
Wang et al., 2024
develop Tucker low-rank and envelope-based discriminant structures under group sparsity. Against this backdrop, we develop CP low-rank tensor LDA under a TGMM with common mode-wise covariances and extend it to semiparametric tensor LDA with a learned Gaussianizing flow, providing non-asymptotic misclassification bounds that account for both low-rank structure and latent distributional mismatch.

1.2
Notation and Paper Organization

Let
[M]=\{1,\ldots,M\}
and
\mathbbm{1}(\cdot)
denote the indicator function. For matrix
\boldsymbol{A}=(a_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, the Frobenius and spectral norms are
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{\rm F}=(\sum_{ij}a_{ij}^{2})^{1/2}
and
\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{2}
, respectively. For squared matrix
\boldsymbol{A}=(a_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, denote its smallest and largest eigenvalues as
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{A})
and
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{A})
, respectively. For sequences
\{a_{n}\}
and
\{b_{n}\}
, we write
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if
|a_{n}|\leq C|b_{n}|
,
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if
1/C\leq a_{n}/b_{n}\leq C
, and
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}/b_{n}=0
, for some constant
C
and sufficiently large
n
. Similarly,
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
(resp.
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
) means
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
(resp.
a_{n}\geq Cb_{n}
).

An
M
-th order tensor is a multi-dimensional array
{\cal X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
, with index
{\cal I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{M})
. The inner product
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\sum_{{\cal I}}{\cal X}_{{\cal I}}{\cal Y}_{{\cal I}}
induces the Frobenius norm
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\rm F}=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}\rangle^{1/2}
. For
\mathnormal{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{\widetilde{d_{m}}\times d_{m}}
, the mode-
m
product
\mathcal{X}\times_{m}\mathnormal{A}
yields an
M
-th order tensor of size
d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m-1}\times\widetilde{d_{m}}\times d_{m+1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}
, where
(\mathcal{X}\times_{m}A)_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m-1},j,i_{m+1},\ldots,i_{M}}=\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{d_{m}}\mathcal{X}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}A_{j,i_{m}}
. The mode-
m
matricization
\text{mat}_{m}(\mathcal{X})
reshapes
{\cal X}
into a
d_{m}\times\prod_{k\neq m}d_{k}
matrix, where element
\mathcal{X}_{i_{1}\dots i_{M}}
maps to position
(i_{m},j)
, with
j=1+\sum\nolimits_{k\neq m}(i_{k}-1)\prod_{l<k,l\neq m}d_{l}
. For
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,M\}
, the multi-mode matricization
{\rm mat}_{S}({\cal X})
yields a
\prod_{m\in S}d_{m}
-by-
\prod_{m\notin S}d_{m}
matrix with row index

i=1+\sum_{m\in S}(i_{m}-1)\prod_{\ell\in S,\ell<m}d_{\ell}
and column index

j=1+\sum_{m\notin S}(i_{m}-1)\prod_{\ell\notin S,\ell<m}d_{\ell}
. Vectorization is denoted
\text{vec}(\mathcal{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d=\prod_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}
.

The Tensor Normal distribution generalizes matrix normal to higher-orders
(
Hoff, 2011
)
. For
{\cal Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
i.i.d.
N(0,1)
elements, mean tensor
\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
and mode covariance matrices
\Sigma_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}}
for
m\in[M]
, define
{\cal X}={\cal M}+{\cal Z}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{1/2}
.
Then,
{\cal X}
follows a tensor normal distribution
{\cal X}\sim{\cal T}{\cal N}({\cal M};\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}:=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
, which is equivalent to
\vect({\cal X})\sim{\cal N}(\vect({\cal M});\Sigma_{M}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sigma_{1})
, where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product.

Organization.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces tensor discriminant analysis with a CP low-rank discriminant tensor, including
rc-PCA
initialization and an iterative refinement procedure. Section
3
establishes theoretical guarantees with estimation error bounds and minimax-optimal misclassification risk rates. Section
4
proposes semiparametric CP-TDA with a learned Gaussianizing flow based on deep neural networks. Section
5
reports simulation studies, and Section
6
illustrates empirical performance on real world data. Section
7
concludes. All proofs, lemmas and additional experiments are deferred to the supplementary material.

2  CP Tensor Discriminant Analysis: The Parametric Model

In this section, we develop the parametric foundation of our approach by studying tensor discriminant analysis under the Tensor Gaussian Mixture Model. We introduce a CP low-rank structure for the discriminant tensor and formulate the resulting CP-structured Tensor LDA (CP-TDA) estimator together with its high-dimensional estimation procedure. This parametric framework serves as the theoretical backbone of our methodology and provides key structural insights (low-rank discriminants, initialization requirements, and contraction behavior) that enable the semiparametric representation-learning extension with neural network encoders and flows in Section
4
.

2.1
Tensor Gaussian Mixture Model and Oracle Tensor LDA

We consider a high-dimensional tensor predictor
{\cal X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\cdots\times d_{M}}
and class label
Y\in[K]
under the Tensor Gaussian Mixture Model (TGMM)
(
Mai et al., 2021
)
,

({\cal X}\;|\;Y=k)\sim{\cal T}{\cal N}({\cal M}_{k};\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}),\quad\pi_{k}:=\mathbb{P}(Y=k),\quad\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}=1,\quad\text{for any }k\in[K],

(1)

where
{\cal M}_{k}
is the class mean tensor,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
represents within-class covariance matrices, and
0<\pi_{k}<1
is the prior probability for class
k
.
We focus on binary classification (
K=2
) with common covariance
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.
Given independent samples from the two tensor-normal distributions, the goal is to classify future observations.

When parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}:=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},{\cal M}_{1},{\cal M}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
are known, the Bayes-optimal tensor LDA rule is:

\Upsilon({\cal X})=\mathbbm{1}\big\{\langle{\cal X}-{\cal M},\;{\cal B}\rangle+\log(\pi_{2}/\pi_{1})\geq 0\big\},

(2)

where
\mathbbm{1}(\cdot)
is the indicator function,
{\cal M}=({\cal M}_{1}+{\cal M}_{2})/2
,
{\cal D}=({\cal M}_{2}-{\cal M}_{1})
, and the discriminant tensor
{\cal B}={\cal D}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{-1}
.
This generalizes classical vector LDA to the tensor setting and is optimal under TGMM
(
Mai et al., 2021
)
. The optimal misclassification error is

R_{\rm opt}=\pi_{1}\phi(\Delta^{-1}\log(\pi_{2}/\pi_{1})-\Delta/2)+\pi_{2}(1-\phi(\Delta^{-1}\log(\pi_{2}/\pi_{1})+\Delta/2)),

where
\phi
is the standard normal CDF and
\Delta=\sqrt{\langle{\cal B},\;{\cal D}\rangle}
is the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR).

Classical vector LDA can be recovered by vectorizing
{\cal X}
but incurs severe computational and statistical burdens.
Existing high-dimensional LDA methods impose sparsity on a vectorized discriminant, ignoring multiway structure and becoming infeasible even for moderate tensor dimensions
(
Mai et al., 2012
;
Cai and Zhang, 2019
)
; for instance, the adaptive procedure in
Cai and Zhang, 2019
involves linear programs with 27,000 constraints for a
30\times 30\times 30
tensor.
Existing tensor discriminant analysis methods impose sparsity on the discriminant tensor
(
Pan et al., 2019
)
or Tucker low-rank tensor envelope
(
Wang et al., 2024
)
, but none model a CP low-rank discriminant tensor.

Empirical evidence shows that discriminative signals in tensor data often concentrate along a few multilinear directions
(see
Wen et al., 2024
, and our empirical results)
. When class separation is driven by specific discriminative directions in each mode, their multilinear interaction naturally forms a rank-1 outer product structure: (
w\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\circ\boldsymbol{a}_{2}\circ\cdots\circ\boldsymbol{a}_{M}
), where
\boldsymbol{a}_{m}
identifies the key direction in mode
m
and
\circ
denotes the tensor outer product. When multiple discriminative patterns exist, this motivates a CP low-rank structure with rank
R\ll d_{m}
for the discriminant tensor:

{\cal B}=\sum\nolimits_{r=1}^{R}w_{r}\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}_{r1}\circ\boldsymbol{a}_{r2}\circ\cdots\circ\boldsymbol{a}_{rM}),

(3)

where each rank-1 component captures one multilinear pattern,
w_{r}>0
represents its signal strength, and
\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}
are unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}}
.
Beyond empirical motivation, low-rank structure is essential for consistent high-dimensional LDA. As shown by
Cai and Zhang, 2021
, no data-driven method can achieve optimal misclassification error
R_{\rm opt}
in high dimensions (
d\gtrsim n
) with unknown means, even given identity covariances. Under the CP low-rank assumption, however, our CP-TDA rule with carefully designed estimation of the discriminant tensor achieves minimax optimal misclassification rates, as established in Theorems
3.3
and
3.4
.

Remark 1
.

While Tucker decomposition
{\cal B}={\cal F}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{M}\boldsymbol{U}_{M}
with core tensor
{\cal F}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}\times\cdots\times r_{M}}
and orthogonal loading matrices
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}\times r_{i}}
offers an alternative low-rank structure, CP provides distinct advantages for discriminant analysis. First, when class separation is driven by specific discriminative directions in each mode, these directions naturally interact through outer products to form rank-1 CP components, making CP the natural decomposition choice. Second, CP offers methodological advantages
(
Han et al., 2024b
;
Erichson et al., 2020
)
: it is uniquely identified up to sign changes and permutations, avoiding Tucker’s ambiguity from invertible transformations of the core tensor and loading matrices. Although CP can be viewed as Tucker with a superdiagonal core, we allow non-orthogonal CP bases
\{\boldsymbol{a}_{rm},r\in[R]\}
, offering greater flexibility than Tucker’s typical orthonormal representation. Moreover, CP’s signal strength sequence is more parsimonious than Tucker’s core tensor, enhancing both interpretability and estimation efficiency.

Remark 2
.

Our framework can readily incorporate covariates in the same way as
Pan et al., 2019
: first regress out covariate effects from the tensor observations, then apply CP-TDA to the residual tensors. The theoretical guarantees in our paper extend naturally to this preprocessing step.

2.2
Estimation of CP Low-Rank Discriminant Tensor

Given the oracle Tensor LDA rule, tensor classification reduces to estimating the discriminant tensor
{\cal B}
from data and plugging the estimate into the rule. The natural sample analogue, obtained from sample means
\overline{{\cal X}}^{(k)}
and estimated mode-wise covariances
\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}
, is

\widehat{\cal B}=(\overline{{\cal X}}^{(2)}-\overline{{\cal X}}^{(1)})\times_{m=1}^{M}\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1},

(4)

where observations
{\cal X}_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,{\cal X}_{n_{k}}^{(k)}
are i.i.d. from class
k=1,2
,
\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}=(n_{1}+n_{2})^{-1}d_{-m}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}

\matk({\cal X}_{i}^{(k)}-\overline{{\cal X}}^{(k)}){\matk}^{\top}({\cal X}_{i}^{(k)}-\overline{{\cal X}}^{(k)})
with
d_{-m}=d/d_{m}
for
m\in[M]
, and for identifiability we rescale
\widehat{\Sigma}_{M}
by a factor
\widehat{C}_{\sigma}^{-1}
with
\widehat{C}_{\sigma}=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\widehat{\Sigma}_{m,11}/\widehat{\Var}({\cal X}_{1,1\cdots 1})
.
With
n=\min(n_{1},n_{2})
, following
Drton et al., 2021
,
\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}
is positive definite and (
4
) is well-defined with probability 1 if
n>d_{m}/d_{-m}
. This condition is mild for comparable mode dimensions.
The sample discriminant tensor
\widehat{\cal B}
serves as a perturbed version of the true
{\cal B}
that can be substantially improved under CP low-rankness (
3
).

Unlike standard tensor decomposition settings, the perturbation error
\widehat{{\cal B}}-{\cal B}
exhibits complex dependence and anisotropy, inherited from cross-sectional dependence among tensor normal distributions and mode-wise covariance inversions. This leads to noisier perturbations and additional analytical difficulty. Moreover, as discussed in the previous subsection, the sample discriminant tensor does not leverage low-rankness and thus cannot achieve optimal misclassification risk.

The next subsections introduce our solution to denoise
\widehat{\cal B}
: a new Randomized Composite PCA (
rc-PCA
) initialization superior to existing CP initialization, followed by an iterative refinement procedure. Together they yield a reliable estimator of the high-dimensional CP low-rank discriminant tensor
{\cal B}
.

2.2.1
rc-PCA
: CP Initialization under Weaker Incoherence

Estimating a CP low-rank discriminant tensor requires an iterative refinement procedure beginning with the sample estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
in (
4
); details appear in Section
2.2.2
.
Because denoising
\widehat{\cal B}
is a highly non-convex problem with exponentially many stationary points
(
Arous et al., 2019
)
, initialization becomes the central bottleneck: without a suitable starting point within a narrow basin of attraction, iterative refinement fails to reach the true discriminant tensor at the optimal statistical rate. The difficulty is heightened here because
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}-\mathcal{B}
exhibits complex dependent noise.

To address this, we introduce Randomized Composite PCA (
rc-PCA
), a new initialization method (Algorithm
1
). By combining randomized projections with composite PCA,
rc-PCA
relaxes the signal-to-noise ratio and incoherence requirements among CP bases, yielding initial CP basis estimates lying within the contraction region of the refinement algorithm. With polynomially many random attempts,
rc-PCA
reaches the attraction ball of the global optimum with high probability (Theorem
3.2
).

Our strategy handles two scenarios: significant gaps between signal strengths
\{w_{r},r\in[R]\}
, and comparable signal strengths. For the multi-mode matricization,

{\rm mat}_{S}({\cal B})=\sum\limits_{r=1}^{R}w_{r}\vect(\circ_{m\in S}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm})\vect(\circ_{m\in S^{c}}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm})^{\top},

where
S,S^{c}
partition
[M]
. For orthogonal tensor components, signal strengths correspond directly to singular values.
For non-orthogonal cases, Proposition
2.1
shows that under mild non-orthogonality (incoherence condition), the difference between signal strengths and singular values remains small, allowing us to use singular value gaps as proxies for signal strength differences.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}_{S}=(\boldsymbol{a}_{1,S},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{R,S})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{S}\times R}
and
\boldsymbol{A}_{S^{c}}=(\boldsymbol{a}_{1,S^{c}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{R,S^{c}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{S^{c}}\times R}
, where
\boldsymbol{a}_{r,S}=\vect(\circ_{m\in S}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm})
and
\boldsymbol{a}_{r,S^{c}}=\vect(\circ_{m\in S^{c}}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm})
. Then

\mathrm{mat}_{S}({\cal B})=\boldsymbol{A}_{S}W\boldsymbol{A}_{S^{c}}^{\top}
with
W=\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{R})
. For
\delta=\|\boldsymbol{A}_{S}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}_{S}-I_{R}\|_{2}\vee\|\boldsymbol{A}_{S^{c}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}_{S^{c}}-I_{R}\|_{2}
and
w_{1}\geq\cdots\geq w_{R}
, the
r
-th largest singular values
\lambda_{r}
of
\mathrm{mat}_{S}({\cal B})
and the signal strengths
w_{r}
satisfy:
|\lambda_{r}-w_{r}|\leq\sqrt{2}\delta w_{1}.

Algorithm
1
first applies Composite PCA (CPCA) for large-gap scenarios. When CPCA fails due to insufficient spectral gaps, it employs Procedure
2
, which (1) performs random projections to enlarge the gap of the leading singular value from the rest, enabling CPCA to extract candidate CP bases associated with the dominant component, and (2) clusters these candidates to obtain representative CP bases. As shown in Appendix
E.1
, polynomially many random projections ensure coverage of all desirable initializations with high probability and provable bounds.

Input :
Initial tensor
\widehat{\cal B}
, CP rank
R
,
S\subset[M]
, small constant
0<c_{0}<1
.

1

If
S=\emptyset
, pick
S\subset[M]
to maximize
\min(d_{S},d/d_{S})
with
d_{S}=\prod_{m\in S}d_{m}
and
d=\prod_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}
.

2

Unfold
\widehat{\cal B}
to be a
d_{S}\times(d/d_{S})
matrix
{\rm mat}_{S}(\widehat{\cal B})
.

3

Compute
\widehat{\lambda}_{r},\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{r},\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{r}
,
1\leq r\leq R
as the top
R
components in the SVD
{\rm mat}_{S}(\widehat{\cal B})=\sum_{r}\widehat{\lambda}_{r}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{r}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{r}^{\top}
. Set
\widehat{\lambda}_{0}=\infty
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{R+1}=0
.

4

if

\min\{|\widehat{\lambda}_{r}-\widehat{\lambda}_{r-1}|,|\widehat{\lambda}_{r}-\widehat{\lambda}_{r+1}|\}>c_{0}\widehat{\lambda}_{R}

then

5

Compute
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca}
as the top left singular vector of
\matk(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times(d_{S}/d_{m})}
for
m\in S
, or
\matk(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times(d_{S^{c}}/d_{m})}
for
m\in S^{c}
.

else

6

Form disjoint index sets
I_{1},...,I_{N}
from all contiguous indices
1\leq r\leq R
that do not satisfy the above criteria of the eigengap.

7

For each
I_{j}
, form
d_{S}\times(d/d_{S})
matrix
\Xi_{j}=\sum_{\ell\in I_{j}}\widehat{\lambda}_{\ell}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell}^{\top}
, reshape it into a tensor
\Xi_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
. Then run Procedure
2
on
\Xi_{j}
to obtain
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca}
for all
r\in I_{j},m\in[M]
.

8

Output :
Warm initialization
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca},1\leq r\leq R,1\leq m\leq M
.

Algorithm 1

Randomized Composite PCA (
rc-PCA
)

Input :
Noisy tensor
\Xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
, rank
s
,
S_{1}\subset[M]\backslash\{1\}
, number of random projections
L
, tuning parameter
\nu
.

1

If
S_{1}=\emptyset
, pick
S_{1}
to maximize
\min(d_{S_{1}},d_{-1}/d_{S_{1}})
with
d_{S_{1}}=\prod_{m\in S_{1}}d_{m}
and
d_{-1}=\prod_{m=2}^{M}d_{m}
. Let
S_{1}\vee S_{1}^{c}=[M]\backslash\{1\}
.

2

for

\ell=1
to
L

do

3

Randomly draw a standard Gaussian vector
\theta\sim{\cal N}(0,I_{d_{1}})
.

4

Compute
\Xi\times_{1}\theta
, unfold it to be
d_{S_{1}}\times(d_{-1}/d_{S_{1}})
matrix, and compute its leading singular value
\eta_{\ell}
and singular vector
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell},\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell}
.

5

Compute
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell m}
as the top left singular vector of
\matk(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times(d_{S_{1}}/d_{m})}
for
m\in S_{1}
, or
\matk(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times(d_{S_{1}^{c}}/d_{m})}
for
m\in S_{1}^{c}
.

6

Compute
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell 1}=\Xi\times_{m=2}^{M}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell m}/\left\lVert\Xi\times_{m=2}^{M}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell m}\right\rVert_{2}
.

for

r=1
to
s

do

7

Among
\ell\in[L]
, choose
\ell^{*}
with tuple
(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell^{*}m},1\leq m\leq M)
that correspond to the largest
\|\Xi\times_{m=1}^{M}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell m}\|_{2}
. Set it to be
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca}=\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell^{*}m}
.

8

Remove all
\ell\in[L]
with
\max_{1\leq m\leq M}|\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell m}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca}|>\nu
.

9

Output :
Warm initialization
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca},1\leq r\leq s,1\leq m\leq M
.

Procedure 2

Randomized Projection

2.2.2
Iterative Refinement of the CP Low-Rank Discriminant Tensor

Our refinement procedure employs iterative tensor projection (Algorithm
3
). Each iteration performs simultaneous orthogonalized projections across tensor modes to refine the CP components
\boldsymbol{a}_{rm},\;r\in[R]
, followed by signal strength estimation
w_{r},\;r\in[R]
. This approach preserves the signal strength of
{\cal B}
while reducing the perturbation error
\widehat{\cal B}-{\cal B}
, thereby improving the signal-to-noise ratio and estimation accuracy.

To illustrate, consider estimating mode-
m
CP bases
\{\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\}_{r\in[R]}
given true mode-
\ell
CP bases
\{\boldsymbol{a}_{r\ell}\}_{r\in[R]}
for all
\ell\neq m
. Define
\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell}=[\boldsymbol{a}_{1\ell},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{R\ell}]
and its right inverse
\boldsymbol{B}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell})^{-1}=[\boldsymbol{b}_{1\ell},...,\boldsymbol{b}_{R\ell}]
, where
\boldsymbol{a}_{k\ell}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b}_{r\ell}=\mathbbm{1}\{k=r\}
. Projecting the noisy
\widehat{\cal B}
with
\boldsymbol{b}_{r\ell}
on all modes
\ell\neq m
isolates the signal component containing only
\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}
:

\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}=\widehat{\cal B}\times_{\ell=1,\ell\neq m}^{M}\boldsymbol{b}_{r\ell}^{\top}=\underbrace{w_{r}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}}_{\text{signal}}+\underbrace{(\widehat{\cal B}-{\cal B})\times_{\ell=1,\ell\neq m}^{M}\boldsymbol{b}_{r\ell}^{\top}}_{\text{noise}}.

(5)

The signal retains strength
w_{r}
along
\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}
, while noise is filtered through projections. When the true
\{\boldsymbol{B}_{\ell},\;\ell\neq m\}
are known, the noise term is small relative to the signal, enabling signal recovery with theoretical guarantees via perturbation theory
(
Wedin, 1972
)
. In practice, with unknown
\{\boldsymbol{B}_{\ell},\;\ell\neq m\}
, we iteratively update CP bases
\{\boldsymbol{a}_{rm},\;r\in[R],m\in[M]\}
at iteration
t
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\cal B}\times_{1}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r1}^{(t)\top}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{m-1}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r,m-1}^{(t)\top}\times_{m+1}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r,m+1}^{(t-1)\top}\times_{m+2}\cdots\times_{M}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rM}^{(t-1)\top},\quad\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t)}=\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}^{(t)}/\|\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}^{(t)}\|_{2}.

The projection error becomes

\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}-\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}^{(t)}=w_{r}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}-\sum_{i=1}^{R}\widetilde{w}_{i,r}\boldsymbol{a}_{im}+\boldsymbol{e}_{rm}-\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{e}_{rm},

where
\widetilde{w}_{i,r}=w_{i}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}[\boldsymbol{a}_{i,\ell}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r\ell}^{(t)}]\prod_{\ell=m+1}^{M}[\boldsymbol{a}_{i,\ell}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r\ell}^{(t-1)}]
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{rm}=(\widehat{\cal B}-{\cal B})\times_{\ell=1}^{M}\boldsymbol{b}_{r,\ell}^{\top}
, and
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{e}_{rm}=(\widehat{\cal B}-{\cal B})\times_{\ell=1}^{m-1}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r,\ell}^{(t)\top}\times_{\ell=m+1}^{M}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r,\ell}^{(t-1)\top}
.
The multiplicative measure
\widetilde{w}_{i,r}
decays rapidly as
\boldsymbol{a}_{i,\ell}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r\ell}^{(t)}
approaches zero for
i\neq r
with increasing iterations, since
\widetilde{w}_{i,r}
contains
M-1
multiplicative terms. The remaining projected noise is controlled by the signal-to-noise ratio, allowing us to establish upper bounds on estimation error and ensure accuracy of the recovered CP bases.

Input :
Initial tensor
\widehat{\cal B}
, CP rank
R
, warm-start
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(0)},1\leq r\leq R,1\leq m\leq M
, tolerance parameter
\epsilon>0
, maximum number of iterations
T

1

Let
t=0
, and compute
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{1m}^{(0)},\ldots,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rm}^{(0)})
as the right inverse of
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{1m}^{(0)},\ldots,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(0)})^{\top}
.

2

repeat

3

Set
t=t+1
.

4

for

m=1
to
M

do

5

for

r=1
to
R

do

6

Compute
\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}^{(t)}=\widehat{\cal B}\times_{1}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r1}^{(t)\top}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{m-1}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r,m-1}^{(t)\top}\times_{m+1}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{r,m+1}^{(t-1)\top}\times_{m+2}\cdots\times_{M}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rM}^{(t-1)\top}

7

Compute
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t)}=\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}^{(t)}/\|\boldsymbol{z}_{rm}^{(t)}\|_{2}

Compute
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{1m}^{(t)},\ldots,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rm}^{(t)})
as the right inverse of
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{1m}^{(t)},\ldots,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t)})^{\top}

8

Set
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{1m}^{(t+1)},...,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rm}^{(t+1)})=(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{1m}^{(t)},...,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rm}^{(t)})

Compute
\widehat{w}_{r}^{(t)}=\big|\widehat{\cal B}\times_{m=1}^{M}(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rm}^{(t)})^{\top}\big|,1\leq r\leq R

until

t=T

or

\max_{r,m}\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t)}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t)\top}-\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t-1)}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t-1)\top}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon
;

9

Output :

\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}=\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(t)}
,

\widehat{w}_{r}=\big|\widehat{\cal B}\times_{m=1}^{M}(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{b}_{rm}^{(t)})^{\top}\big|
,

\widehat{\cal B}^{\rm cp}=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\widehat{w}_{r}\circ_{m=1}^{M}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}
,
1\leq r\leq R,m\leq M

Algorithm 3

Discriminant Tensor Iterative Projection for CP low-rank (DISTIP-CP)

Remark 3

(CP-TDA Rule)
.

Finally, given the estimated discriminant tensor
\widehat{\cal B}^{\rm cp}
, the high-dimensional CP-TDA rule is given by

\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}({\cal X}^{*})=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\langle{\cal X}^{*}-(\overline{{\cal X}}^{(1)}+\overline{{\cal X}}^{(2)})/2,\;\widehat{\cal B}^{\rm cp}\rangle+\log(\widehat{\pi}_{2}/\widehat{\pi}_{1})\geq 0\right\}.

(6)

This assigns
{\cal X}^{*}
to class
1
when
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}({\cal X}^{*})=0
or
2
when
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}({\cal X}^{*})=1
.

3  Theoretical Analysis of CP-TDA

In this section, we establish the statistical properties of our algorithms, providing consistency guarantees and statistical error rates for the estimated discriminant tensor and misclassification error under regularity conditions. We define
d=\prod_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}
,
d_{-m}=d/d_{m}
,
d_{\min}=\min\{d_{1},\cdots,d_{M}\}
. We measure the distance between estimated and true CP bases using
\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}{\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\top}\|_{2}=\sqrt{1-(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm})^{2}}
. Assume
w_{1}\geq w_{2}\geq\cdots\geq w_{R}
. As
\|\boldsymbol{a}_{im}\|_{2}^{2}=1
, we quantify the correlation among columns of
\boldsymbol{A}_{m}
as

\displaystyle\delta_{m}=\|\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}_{m}-I_{R}\|_{2},\quad\text{with}\quad\delta_{\max}=\max\{\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{M}\}.

(7)

Global convergence.
We first analyze the global convergence of Algorithm
3
, given a suitable warm initialization.
Define the following quantities in terms of the initialization error
\psi_{0}
:

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(1-\delta_{\max})(1-1/(4R))}-(R^{1/2}+1)\psi_{0},

(8)

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=2\alpha^{1-M}\sqrt{R-1}(w_{1}/w_{R})\psi_{0}^{M-2},

(9)

\displaystyle\psi^{\rm ideal}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{M}d_{k}}}{\sqrt{n}w_{r}}+\frac{w_{1}}{w_{r}}\max_{1\leq k\leq M}\sqrt{\frac{d_{k}}{nd_{-k}}},\quad\psi^{\rm ideal}=\psi^{\rm ideal}_{R}.

(10)

The following theorem provides the convergence rates for the CP low-rank discriminant tensor.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose
C_{0}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\otimes_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\otimes_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m})\leq C_{0}
for some constant
C_{0}>0
. Assume
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
and
n=\min\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
.
Let
\Omega_{0}=\{\underset{r\in[R],m\in[M]}{\max}\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(0)}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(0)\top}-\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\top})\|_{2}\leq\psi_{0}\}
for any initial estimates
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{(0)}
. Suppose
\alpha>0
,
\rho<1
, with the quantities defined in (
8
) and (
9
).
After at most
T=O(\log\log(\psi_{0}/\psi^{\rm ideal}))
iterations, Algorithm
3
produces final estimates satisfying

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{rm}\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{rm}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\top}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\psi^{\rm ideal}_{r},\quad\left\lvert\widehat{w}_{r}-w_{r}\right\rvert\leq Cw_{r}\psi^{\rm ideal}_{r},

(11)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\cal B}^{\rm cp}-{\cal B}\right\rVert_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{M}d_{k}R}}{\sqrt{n}}+Cw_{1}\sqrt{R}\max_{1\leq k\leq M}\sqrt{\frac{d_{k}}{nd_{-k}}},

(12)

with probability at least
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{0})-n^{-c}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp(-cd_{m})
.

The statistical convergence rates in (
11
), (
12
) comprise two components: the first matches conventional tensor CP decomposition rates, while the second reflects the estimation accuracy of the mode-
k
sample precision matrices
\widehat{\Sigma}_{k}^{-1}
. By updating each CP basis vector
\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}
separately, our algorithm removes bias from non-orthogonal loading vectors.
Our theoretical analysis employs large deviation inequalities and spectrum perturbation theory
(
Cai and Zhang, 2018
;
Han et al., 2024a
)
to analyze the projected discriminant tensor. Notably, we accommodate correlated noise tensor entries, unlike most existing tensor decomposition work assuming i.i.d. entries
(
Zhang and Xia, 2018
;
Wang and Li, 2020
)
. The proof appears in Section
E.2
.

Remark 4
.

In Theorem
3.1
, the initialization accuracy
\psi_{0}
must be sufficiently small to ensure
\alpha>0
,
\rho<1
, which impose sharper quantitative requirements on the warm start. The condition
\alpha>0
requires
R^{1/2}\psi_{0}
to be small, with the extra
R^{1/2}
factor arising from inverting the estimated
A_{m}^{\top}A_{m}
in constructing mode-
m
projections. The condition
\rho<1
ensures that the update map is contractive in a neighborhood of the true CP bases, so the iterates stay within this basin of attraction and the error decays geometrically down to
\psi^{\text{ideal}}
.

Convergence of initialization.

Theorem
3.2
establishes performance bounds for
rc-PCA
initialization (Algorithm
1
), depending on CP basis incoherence (the degree of non-orthogonality). We consider two complementary regimes: (i) large spectral separation and (ii) small spectral separation, where the large-separation analysis no longer applies.
Recall
\boldsymbol{A}_{m}=(\boldsymbol{a}_{1m},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{Rm})
. We measure correlation via

\displaystyle\delta=\|\boldsymbol{A}_{S}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}_{S}-I_{R}\|_{2}\vee\|\boldsymbol{A}_{S^{c}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}_{S^{c}}-I_{R}\|_{2},

(13)

where
\boldsymbol{A}_{S}=(\boldsymbol{a}_{1,S},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{R,S})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{S}\times R}
and
\boldsymbol{A}_{S^{c}}=(\boldsymbol{a}_{1,S^{c}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{R,S^{c}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{S^{c}}\times R}
, with
S
maximizing
\min(d_{S},d_{S^{c}})
,
d_{S}=\prod_{m\in S}d_{m},d_{S^{c}}=\prod_{m\in S^{c}}d_{m}
, and
\boldsymbol{a}_{r,S}=\vect(\circ_{m\in S}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}),\boldsymbol{a}_{r,S^{c}}=\vect(\circ_{m\in S^{c}}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm})
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose
C_{0}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\otimes_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\otimes_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m})\leq C_{0}
for some constant
C_{0}>0
. Assume
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
and
n=\min\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
.

(i) Large spectral separation. If
\min\{w_{r}-w_{r+1},w_{r}-w_{r-1}\}\geq c_{0}w_{R}
for all
1\leq r\leq R
, with
w_{0}=\infty,w_{R+1}=0
, and
c_{0}
is sufficiently small constant, then with probability at least
1-n^{-c}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}\exp(-cd_{m})
,
rc-PCA
(Algorithm
1
) yields the following error bounds,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca\top}-\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\top}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\big(1+2\sqrt{2}(w_{1}/w_{R})\big)\delta+C\phi_{1},

(14)

for all
1\leq r\leq R
,
1\leq m\leq M
, where
C
is some positive constants, and

\displaystyle\phi_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{d_{S}}+\sqrt{d_{S^{c}}}}{\sqrt{n}w_{R}}+\frac{w_{1}}{w_{R}}\max_{1\leq k\leq M}\sqrt{\frac{d_{k}}{nd_{-k}}}.

(15)

(ii) Small spectral separation. When condition (i) fails, assume
w_{1}\asymp w_{R}
and the number of random projections
L\geq C_{0}d_{1}^{2}\vee C_{0}d_{1}R^{2(w_{1}/w_{R})^{2}}
. Then with probability at least
1-n^{-c}-d_{1}^{-c}-\sum_{m=2}^{M}e^{-cd_{m}}
,
rc-PCA
(Algorithm
1
) yields the following error bounds,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\rm rcpca\top}-\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\top}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\delta_{\max}}+C\sqrt{\phi_{2}}.

(16)

where
C
is some positive constants, and

\displaystyle\phi_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{d_{1}d_{S_{1}}}+\sqrt{d_{1}d_{S_{1}^{c}}}}{\sqrt{n}w_{R}}+\frac{w_{1}}{w_{R}}\max_{1\leq k\leq M}\sqrt{\frac{d_{k}}{nd_{-k}}}.

(17)

The bounds in (
14
) and (
16
) comprise bias from non-orthogonal loading vectors (first term) and stochastic error from noise and precision matrices estimations (second term). When the eigengap condition is not met, randomized projection yields the slower rate in (
16
) compared to (
14
). For case (ii), a generalized result for arbitrary eigen ratios
w_{1}/w_{R}
appears in Section
E.1
.

Using Algorithm
1
to initialize Algorithm
3
, with
\psi_{0}
as the maximum of the right-hand sides of (
14
) or (
16
), the condition in Theorem
3.1
satisfies with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{0})\geq 1-n^{-c}-d_{1}^{-c}-\sum_{m=2}^{M}e^{-cd_{m}}
.

Remark 5
.

Our initialization advances existing tensor CP methods in several ways. First, unlike
Han and Zhang, 2023
;
Han et al., 2024b
, we handle repeated singular values in tensor CP decomposition. Second,
rc-PCA
requires weaker incoherence conditions for the non-orthogonality of the CP basis vectors under small CP rank. For example, for 3-way tensors,
Anandkumar et al., 2014b
requires incoherence condition
\max_{i\neq j}\max_{m}\boldsymbol{a}_{im}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}_{jm}\leq{\rm polylog}(d_{\min})/\sqrt{d_{\min}}
, while our condition
\psi_{0}\lesssim 1
yields
\max_{i\neq j}\max_{m}\boldsymbol{a}_{im}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}_{jm}\lesssim R^{-2}
, which is weaker when
R\lesssim d_{\min}^{1/4}
.

Misclassification error.

We now establish the misclassification error rates for high-dimensional tensor LDA with CP low-rank structure. We derive both upper and lower bounds for the excess misclassification risk, demonstrating that Algorithm
3
achieves optimal rates.

Under the TGMM and CP-TDA rule defined in (
6
), the classifier’s performance is measured by its misclassification error

\displaystyle{\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})=\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\big({\rm label}({\cal X}^{*})\neq\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}({\cal X}^{*})\big),

where
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
denotes the probability with respect to
{\cal X}^{*}\sim\pi_{1}{\cal T}{\cal N}({\cal M}_{1};\boldsymbol{\Sigma})+\pi_{2}{\cal T}{\cal N}({\cal M}_{2};\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
and
{\rm label}({\cal X}^{*})
denotes the true class of
{\cal X}^{*}
. In this paper, we use the excess misclassification risk relative to the optimal misclassification error,
{\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, to measure the performance of the classifier
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}
.

Theorem 3.3

(Upper bound of misclassification rate)
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
3.1
hold, and
w_{1}\sqrt{\max_{m}d_{m}^{2}R/(nd)}+\sqrt{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R/n}=o(\Delta)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where the SNR is
\Delta=\sqrt{\langle{\cal B},\;{\cal D}\rangle}
.

(i) For bounded
\Delta\leq c_{0}
with some
c_{0}>0
, then with probability at least
1-n^{-c}-d_{1}^{-c}-\sum_{m=2}^{M}e^{-cd_{m}}
, the misclassification rate of classifier
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}
satisfies

{\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq C\left(\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R}{n}\right),

(18)

for some constants
C,c>0
.

(ii) For
\Delta\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, then there exists
\vartheta_{n}=o(1)
, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}-d_{1}^{-c}-\sum_{m=2}^{M}e^{-cd_{m}}
, the misclassification rate of classifier
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}
satisfies

{\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq C\exp\left\{-\left(\frac{1}{8}+\vartheta_{n}\right)\Delta^{2}\right\}\left(\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R}{n}\right),

(19)

for some constants
C,c>0
.

Compared with Theorem
3.1
, the excess misclassification rates in (
18
) and (
19
) only contain one part. The error induced by the estimation accuracy of the mode-
m
precision matrix becomes negligible in the excess misclassification rates.

To understand the difficulty of the tensor classification problem, it is essential to obtain the minimax lower bounds for the excess misclassification risk. We especially consider the following parameter space of CP low-rank discriminant tensors,

\displaystyle{\cal H}=\Big\{\;

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}=({\cal M}_{1},{\cal M}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}):{\cal M}_{1},{\cal M}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}},\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M},\;\Sigma_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}},\;\text{for some}\;C_{0}>0,

\displaystyle C_{0}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\otimes_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\otimes_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m})\leq C_{0},\;{\cal B}=\sum_{r=1}^{R}w_{r}\circ_{m=1}^{M}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\;\text{with}\;\|\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\|_{2}=1\Big\}.

The following lower bound holds over
{\cal H}
.

Theorem 3.4

(Lower bound of misclassification rate)
.

Under the TGMM, the minimax risk of excess misclassification error over the parameter space
{\cal H}
satisfies the following conditions.

(i) For
c_{1}<\Delta\leq c_{2}
with constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, for any
\gamma>0
, there exists constant
C_{\gamma}>0
such that

\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in{\cal H}}\mathbb{P}\left({\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\geq C_{\gamma}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R}{n}\right)\geq 1-\gamma.

(20)

(ii) For
\Delta\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, for any
\gamma>0
, there exists constant
C_{\gamma}>0
and
\vartheta_{n}=o(1)
such that

\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in{\cal H}}\mathbb{P}\left({\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\geq C_{\gamma}\exp\left\{-\left(\frac{1}{8}+\vartheta_{n}\right)\Delta^{2}\right\}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R}{n}\right)\geq 1-\gamma.

(21)

Comparing the upper bounds of the excess misclassification risk (
18
)–(
19
) with lower bounds (
20
)–(
21
), our convergence rates are minimax optimal.

4  Semiparametric CP-TDA via Representation Learning

Although TGMM provides a clean parametric setting where CP-TDA is optimal, real data such as images, graphs, and multimodal observations do not naturally follow tensor-normal structure. Existing semiparametric mixture models based on coordinatewise monotone transformations
(
Liu et al., 2012
;
Han et al., 2013
)
are too restrictive for modern high-dimensional data. To extend CP-TDA to general settings, we propose a new semiparametric approach where a neural network encoder first maps inputs into tensor-valued features, and a tensor Gaussianizing flow then transforms these features into a latent space that closely approximates TGMM. CP-TDA is applied to this latent representation, combining the expressive power of deep learning with the robustness, parsimony, and interpretability of CP-TDA, and enabling improved empirical performance while preserving theoretical guarantees of the discriminant model. We call this Semiparametric CP-TDA framework Semiparametric Tensor Discriminant Networks (STDN).

Architecture Overview.
STDN augments the parametric CP-TDA framework with three components.
First, a tensor encoder
h_{\beta}
maps raw observations
{\cal Z}
with class labels
Y\in[K]
(e.g., images, graphs, text, or vectors) into tensor-valued features
{\cal X}_{\beta}({\cal Z})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
using architecture-agnostic modules (e.g., convolutional, graph-based or transformer-style) whose role is purely representational, producing multiway features that preserve the structural richness of the input domain.

Second, a new tensor Gaussianizing flow
g_{\varphi}
(FlowTGMM) transforms encoder outputs into latent tensor
{\cal X}^{(L)}=g_{\varphi}({\cal X}_{\beta}({\cal Z}))\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
whose class-conditional distributions approximate TGMM. Built on the tensor extension of RealNVP flow
(
Dinh et al., 2017
)
, FlowTGMM learns flexible, mode-aware, class-conditioned transformations mapping encoder features to a latent space where each class approximately follows a tensor normal distribution with common mode-wise covariances.

Finally, the CP-TDA head applies CP low-rank discriminant analysis from Section
2
to the latent tensors. Unlike fully connected softmax classifiers, it imposes parsimonious multilinear structure with
O(R\sum_{m}d_{m})
parameters, yielding mode-wise interpretability and sample efficiency. This creates a semiparametric model where deep neural networks learn features while the classifier maintains statistically optimal CP low-rank discriminant structure rather than a generic softmax layer.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first tensor classifier combining deep representation learning and flow-based deep generative models with a statistically optimal low-rank tensor discriminant analysis, achieving both neural network flexibility and rigorous theoretical guarantees.

4.1
Tensor Gaussianizing Flow (FlowTGMM)

Encoder outputs
\mathcal{X}_{\beta}(\mathcal{Z})
rarely follow TGMM naturally. In practice, they exhibit heterogeneous feature scales, non-Gaussian tails, and complex local geometry that violate the tensor-normal assumption required for CP-TDA’s optimality. To bridge this gap, we propose FlowTGMM, a learnable transformation that maps encoder outputs into a latent tensor space
\mathcal{X}^{(L)}=g_{\varphi}(\mathcal{X}_{\beta}(\mathcal{Z}))\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
whose class-conditional distributions closely approximate TGMM. This alignment enables the CP-TDA head to operate in precisely the setting where it achieves statistical efficiency, interpretability, and minimax-optimal misclassification rates.

Specifically, FlowTGMM adopts Tensor RealNVP (Definition
4.1
), a tensor-structured extension of RealNVP
(
Dinh et al., 2017
)
that uses mode-wise invertible linear mixing and affine coupling layers to preserve multiway structure.
Unlike traditional semiparametric mixture models that rely on restrictive coordinatewise monotone transformations, FlowTGMM learns rich, nonlinear, mode-aware transformations that adapt to the complexity of high-dimensional tensor data while maintaining computational tractability and theoretical rigor.

Definition 4.1

(Tensor RealNVP)
.

Consider a pre-specified binary mask tensor
{\cal K}^{(\ell)}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
with supports
{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}=\{\boldsymbol{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{M}):{\cal K}^{(\ell)}_{\boldsymbol{i}}=1\},\;{\cal A}_{0}^{(\ell)}=\{\boldsymbol{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{M}):{\cal K}^{(\ell)}_{\boldsymbol{i}}=0\}
. Each mapping
g^{(\ell)}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
operates on
{\cal X}^{(\ell-1)}
in two steps: (i) Mode-wise linear mixer
\widetilde{{\cal X}}^{(\ell)}:={\cal X}^{(\ell-1)}\times_{m=1}^{M}\boldsymbol{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}
with pre-specified orthogonal matrices
\boldsymbol{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}}
. (ii) Tensor affine coupling: partition
\widetilde{{\cal X}}^{(\ell)}
into
\widetilde{{\cal X}}_{{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)},\widetilde{{\cal X}}_{{\cal A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
according to the mask. Let
\upsilon^{(\ell)},t^{(\ell)}
be tensor-valued conditioners mapping
\widetilde{{\cal X}}_{{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}
to tensors supported on
{\cal A}_{0}^{(\ell)}
of the same shape. Define

\displaystyle{\cal Y}_{{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}:=\widetilde{{\cal X}}_{{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)},\quad{\cal Y}_{{\cal A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}:=\widetilde{{\cal X}}_{{\cal A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\odot\exp\left(\upsilon^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{{\cal X}}_{{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right)\right)+t^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{{\cal X}}_{{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right),

where
\odot
and
\exp(\cdot)
denote elementwise multiplication and exponentiation. The output is
{\cal X}^{(\ell)}:={\cal Y}_{{\cal A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}+{\cal Y}_{{\cal A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}
. Composing
L
such mappings defines the full Tensor RealNVP flow:

g_{\varphi}:=g^{(L)}\circ\cdots\circ g^{(1)},\quad{\cal X}^{(0)}:={\cal X}_{\beta}({\cal Z}),\quad{\cal X}^{(\ell)}:=g^{(\ell)}({\cal X}^{(\ell-1)}),\ \ell=1,\ldots,L,

with parameters
\varphi=\{\upsilon^{(\ell)},t^{(\ell)}:\ell=1,\ldots,L\}
. The overall flow output is
{\cal X}^{(L)}=g_{\varphi}({\cal X}_{\beta}({\cal Z}))
, which preserves the tensor order and dimensions
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{M})
.

We propose a class-aware flow training scheme that maximizes the TGMM likelihood of latent tensors given class information. Specifically, we train
g_{\varphi}
so that transformed tensor features
{\cal X}^{(L)}=g_{\varphi}({\cal X}_{\beta}({\cal Z}))
approximately follow, conditional on class, a TGMM with common mode-wise covariances
[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
. This yields an objective that combines class-conditional tensor-normal log-likelihood with flow-induced Jacobian correction, encouraging latent representations of each class to concentrate around their respective means with common mode-wise covariances, precisely the setting in which CP-TDA is optimal.

Proposition 4.2

(FlowTGMM)
.

Let
g_{\varphi}=g^{(L)}\circ\cdots\circ g^{(1)}
be the Tensor RealNVP from Definition
4.1
with
{\cal X}_{i}^{(0)}={\cal X}_{\beta}({\cal Z}_{i}),\;{\cal X}_{i}^{(\ell)}=g^{(\ell)}({\cal X}_{i}^{(\ell-1)}),\;\ell=1,\ldots,L
. Let
f(\cdot\mid Y=k;{\cal M}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
denote the tensor normal density with class means
{\cal M}_{k}
and mode-wise covariances
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
, as defined in Section
1.2
. Given a labeled dataset
\{({\cal Z}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{0}}
, the maximum likelihood estimator
\widehat{\varphi}
minimizes

{\cal L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi)=-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\left[\log f({\cal X}_{i}^{(L)}\mid Y=Y_{i};\widehat{\cal M}_{Y_{i}},\widehat{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\log\left|\det\boldsymbol{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}({\cal X}_{i}^{(\ell-1)})\right|\right],

(22)

where
\widehat{\cal M}_{Y_{i}}
and
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are computed from latent features
{\cal X}_{i}^{(L)}
. Here, the log-Jacobian for each mapping
g^{(\ell)}
with orthogonal mode-mixer
\boldsymbol{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}
and affine coupling conditioners
\upsilon^{(\ell)},t^{(\ell)}
, is defined as

\log\left|\det\boldsymbol{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}({\cal X}_{i}^{(\ell-1)})\right|=\sum_{\boldsymbol{j}\in{\cal A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}\upsilon^{(\ell)}(\widetilde{\cal X}_{i,\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)})_{\boldsymbol{j}}.

(23)

Remark 6
.

The sample log-likelihood (
22
) decomposes into two components: (i) the first term
\log f({\cal X}_{i}^{(L)}\mid Y=Y_{i};\widehat{\cal M}_{Y_{i}},\widehat{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
measures how well the latent tensor
{\cal X}_{i}^{(L)}
fits the class-conditional tensor-normal model, encouraging TGMM alignment; (ii) the second term
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\log|\det\boldsymbol{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}({\cal X}_{i}^{(\ell-1)})|
accounts for the flow-induced change of variables, ensuring proper density normalization. Each log-Jacobian in (
23
) consists of affine coupling contributions from
v^{(\ell)}
; the mode-wise mixer contribution vanishes since the mixers are orthogonal, and the term
t^{(\ell)}
disappear because it only affects off-diagonal entries in the triangular Jacobian matrix. See proofs in Appendix
F.1
.

4.2
CP-TDA Cross-Entropy Head

The CP-TDA head operates on latent tensors
\{{\cal X}_{i}^{(L)}\}_{i=1}^{n_{0}}
produced by the encoder-flow pipeline, where
{\cal X}_{i}^{(L)}=g_{\varphi}(h_{\beta}({\cal Z}_{i}))
.
It first computes statistics from the latent tensors as in
Section
2.2
, including class priors
\widehat{\pi}_{k}=n_{k}/(n_{1}+n_{2})
, class means
\widehat{{\cal M}}_{k}^{(L)}
, overall mean
\widehat{{\cal M}}^{(L)}=(\widehat{{\cal M}}_{1}^{(L)}+\widehat{{\cal M}}_{2}^{(L)})/2
, mean difference
\widehat{{\cal D}}^{(L)}=\widehat{{\cal M}}_{2}^{(L)}-\widehat{{\cal M}}_{1}^{(L)}
, mode-wise covariances
\{\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{(L)}\}
, and the sample discriminant tensor
\widehat{{\cal B}}^{(L)}=\widehat{{\cal D}}^{(L)}\times_{m=1}^{M}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{(L)})^{-1}
. It then refines
\widehat{{\cal B}}^{(L)}
via
rc-PCA
initialization followed by DISTIP-CP (Algorithms
1
-
3
), yielding a CP rank-
R
discriminant tensor

\widehat{\cal B}^{{\rm cp},(L)}=\sum_{r=1}^{R}\widehat{w}_{r}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{r1}\circ\cdots\circ\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{rM}\right)
. For input
{\cal Z}^{*}
, the latent representation is
{\cal X}^{*(L)}=g_{\varphi}(h_{\beta}({\cal Z}^{*}))
, and the classifier score is

s_{\beta,\varphi}({\cal Z}^{*})=\left\langle\widehat{\cal B}^{{\rm cp},(L)},{\cal X}^{*(L)}-{\cal M}^{(L)}\right\rangle+\log(\widehat{\pi}_{2}/\widehat{\pi}_{1}),

which follows the decision rule in (
6
).
During training, we construct logits
\{-\frac{1}{2}s_{\beta,\varphi}({\cal Z}^{*}),\frac{1}{2}s_{\beta,\varphi}({\cal Z}^{*})\}
with cross-entropy loss. By constraining the discriminant tensor to CP rank
R
and using common mode-wise covariances, the CP-TDA head achieves substantial parameter efficiency, reducing complexity from
O(\prod_{m}d_{m})
to
O(R\sum_{m}d_{m})
, while providing mode-wise interpretability and maintaining the minimax-optimal guarantees from Section
3
. The complete semiparametric CP-TDA (STDN) is summarized in Algorithm
4
with additional training details provided in Appendix
D
.

4.3
Theoretical Guarantees of Semiparametric CP-TDA (STDN)

Having obtained network parameters
\widehat{\beta}
and
\widehat{\varphi}
from Algorithm
4
, we now analyze the theoretical performance of the resulting classifier. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
denote the true class-conditional distribution of latent tensor
{\cal X}^{(L)}\mid Y=k
with priors
\pi_{k}
, and write
\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}=(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1},\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
. To enable theoretical analysis, we introduce two population benchmarks. First, since the flow may not produce perfect TGMM distributions, we construct a moment-matched TGMM surrogate
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
with class means
{\cal M}_{k}=\mathbb{E}[{\cal X}^{(L)}\mid Y=k]
, common mode-wise covariances
[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
, and priors
\pi_{k}
, yielding oracle LDA with
{\cal D}={\cal M}_{2}-{\cal M}_{1}
, discriminant tensor
{\cal B}={\cal D}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{-1}
, and SNR
\Delta=\sqrt{\langle{\cal B},{\cal D}\rangle}
, as in Section
2.1
. Second, since
\mathcal{B}
may not be CP low-rank while our method constrains to rank
R
for efficiency, we define the oracle CP rank-
R
discriminant tensor as the best rank-
R
approximation:

\mathcal{B}_{R}\in\arg\max_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\in\mathcal{C}_{R}}\langle\mathcal{D},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\|\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{1/2}\|_{F}=\|\mathcal{B}\times_{m}\Sigma_{m}^{1/2}\|_{F},

where
\mathcal{C}_{R}
denotes the cone of CP rank-
R
tensors, yielding SNR
\Delta_{R}=\sqrt{\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{B}_{R}\rangle}
and efficiency loss
\delta_{\rm cp}:=\Delta-\Delta_{R}\geq 0
.
The semiparametric classifier
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
uses the estimated CP rank-
R
discriminant tensor
\widehat{{\cal B}}_{R}^{(L)}
from empirical estimates
\{\widehat{\pi}_{k},\widehat{{\cal M}}_{k}^{(L)},[\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{(L)}]\}
. The following theorem bounds the excess misclassification risk relative to the optimal error
{\cal R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}
.

Theorem 4.3

(Misclassification error rate)
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
3.3
and
Assumption
F.1
in the appendix hold. (i) For bounded
\Delta_{R}\leq\Delta\leq c_{0}
with some
c_{0}>0
, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}-d_{1}^{-c}-\sum_{m=2}^{M}e^{-cd_{m}}
, the misclassification rate of classifier
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\cal R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\leq C\Big(\delta_{\rm cp}+\frac{\sum_{m}d_{m}R}{n}+\Big(\frac{\log n}{n}\Big)^{1/4}\Big),

(24)

where
n=\min\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
and
C,c>0
are constants.

(ii) For
\Delta\geq\Delta_{R}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, then there exists
\vartheta_{n}=o(1)
, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}-d_{1}^{-c}-\sum_{m=2}^{M}e^{-cd_{m}}
, the misclassification rate of classifier
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\cal R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\leq C\Big(\delta_{\rm cp}\ e^{-\big(\frac{(\Delta_{R}/\Delta)^{4}}{8}+\vartheta_{n}\big)\Delta^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R}{n}e^{-(\frac{1}{8}+\vartheta_{n})\Delta_{R}^{2}}+\Big(\frac{\log n}{n}\Big)^{1/4}\Big),

(25)

where
n=\min\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
and
C,c>0
are constants.

The bounds in (
24
) and (
25
) decompose into three terms isolating distinct error sources: (i) population level approximation bias
\delta_{\rm cp}
from restricting the discriminant tensor
{\cal B}
to a CP rank-
R
discriminant tensor
{\cal B}_{R}
, which is typically small in practice; (ii) statistical estimation error, matching the minimax-optimal rate for CP-TDA under TGMM from Theorem
3.3
; and (iii) flow-induced mismatch of order
(\log n/n)^{1/4}
, arising from optimization and generalization error of the FlowTGMM objective. This flow mismatch rate is consistent with conventional rates in flow-based generative models
(
Irons et al., 2022
)
. As shown in Appendix
F.2
, Tensor RealNVP is expressive enough to approximate the latent TGMM arbitrarily well, so no additional approximation error arises beyond this statistical mismatch.

5  Simulation Study

Our simulations confirm the theoretical properties of CP-TDA:
rc-PCA
reliably provides a valid warm start and DISTIP-CP converges to the true CP components.
Under ideal CP low-rank setting, CP-TDA achieves substantially lower estimation and classification errors than existing tensor classifiers. Even when the true discriminant deviates from CP structure, CP-TDA remains superior, demonstrating strong robustness under model misspecification. Additional studies in Appendix
A
examines: (i) heavy-tailed non-Gaussian distributions, (ii) heteroscedastic covariances violating the common-covariance assumption, (iii) rank misspecification and data-driven rank selection, (iv) ultra high-dimensional settings, and (v) class imbalance. Across all settings, CP-TDA maintains superior performance, confirming its practical reliability beyond theoretical assumptions.

Data generation.
We generate data under the TGMM with order-
3
tensors of dimension
(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})

=(30,30,30)
. Each class contains
n_{1}=n_{2}=100
training samples, with
1000
test samples for evaluation. Mode-wise covariances
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{3}
have unit diagonals with off-diagonal elements
3/d_{m}
.
We construct
\{\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\}
using both orthogonal bases and non-orthogonal bases defined by
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{1m}=\boldsymbol{a}_{1m}
and
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}=\frac{\boldsymbol{a}_{1m}+\eta\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}}{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{a}_{1m}+\eta\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\right\rVert_{2}}
for
r\geq 2
, where
\eta=(\vartheta^{-2/M}-1)^{1/2}
,
\vartheta=\delta/(r-1)
and
\delta=0.1
. We examine two eigen-structure regimes: (i) small spectral separation, where all components have equal signal strength
w_{r}\in\{1.5,2.0,2.5\}
; and (ii) large spectral separation, where
w_{r}/w_{r+1}=1.25
with
w_{\rm max}\in\{2,3,4\}
, yielding one dominant component. Class means are
{\cal M}_{1}=0
and
{\cal M}_{2}={\cal B}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}
, where
{\cal B}=\sum_{r=1}^{R}w_{r}a_{r1}\circ\cdots\circ a_{rM}
. Each configuration is repeated 100 times.

rc-PCA
ensures global convergence of DISTIP-CP.

Figure
1
illustrates the behavior of different initialization schemes for DISTIP-CP with non-orthogonal bases under both small and large spectral separation regimes. It displays the logarithm of the CP basis estimation error, measured by
\max_{r,m}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{rm}\widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{rm}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}\boldsymbol{a}_{rm}^{\top}\|_{2}
, across configurations with varying signal strengths.

Alternating Rank-1 Least Squares (ARLS,
Anandkumar et al., 2014b
), the standard CP initialization, fails under small signal strength, where the sample discriminant tensor has a weak signal to noise ratio that violates its assumptions. In such cases, ARLS either diverges or converges to a local optimum, failing to enter the contraction region of the global optimum.

In contrast,
rc-PCA
consistently provides a reliable warm start in all cases, after which DISTIP-CP rapidly converges to the true CP components, matching Theorem
3.2
. Initialization is thus the fundamental bottleneck in discriminant tensor estimation: without a proper warm start, global recovery is unattainable, whereas
rc-PCA
reliably enables it under weaker signal requirements.
This suggests that
rc-PCA
may also benefit other CP-based tensor methods whose performance depends critically on initialization quality.
Figures
2
-
3
in Appendix
A.1
further confirm
rc-PCA
’s reliability under order-3 tensors with orthogonal CP bases and order-4 tensors.

w_{r}
) and (b) large spectral separation (geometric decay with
w_{r}/w_{r+1}=1.25
).

. Across all settings, CP-TDA achieves substantially lower discriminant-tensor estimation error and misclassification error.

) and order-4 tensors with both orthogonal and non-orthogonal bases (Tables

), under both spectral separation regimes. Overall, DISTIP-CP with
rc-PCA
initialization provides uniformly strong performance across signal levels, incoherence regimes, and covariance structures.

Performance under misspecified discriminant structures.

We next evaluate robustness when the true discriminant tensor departs from the ideal CP structure. Two misspecification mechanisms are considered: (i) a Tucker low-rank tensor with controlled off-diagonal energy, and (ii) a CP tensor contaminated by sparse perturbation. Detailed descriptions are provided in Appendix
A.2
.

Tables

-

show misclassification rates and, when available, estimation errors. Across all degrees of misspecification, moderate to large Tucker off-diagonal energy (
\alpha=0.3,0.5,0.7
) and sparse perturbations of varying magnitude (
\tau=0.2,0.4,0.6
), CP-TDA remains the top performer. In the Tucker setting, its misclassification error is substantially lower than that of TuckerLDA, CATCH, GuoSTM, LaiSTDA, ZhouLogitCP, and DATED, even when the true structure is closer to Tucker than to CP. In the CP-plus-sparse setting, CP-TDA again achieves the lowest misclassification rates and estimation errors, while several competing methods deteriorate sharply or fail to recover meaningful structure.

6  Real Data Analysis

We evaluate the proposed Semiparametric Tensor Discriminant Networks (STDN) on graph classification tasks from the widely used D&D and PROTEINS datasets from the
TUDataset
benchmark. These datasets are high-dimensional, structurally rich, and sample-limited, which is precisely the regime where statistical efficiency and principled discriminant modeling are essential.

Our framework employs a tensor neural network encoder to generate multiway graph representations, a tensor Gaussianizing flow that maps these representations into a TGMM-aligned latent space, and a CP-TDA head for final classification. This architecture combines the expressive capacity of learned representations with the parsimony, interpretability, and statistical optimality of CP-TDA, and is encoder-agnostic (any network producing tensor-valued features can be used).

For our experiments, we adopt the state-of-the-art encoder from TTG-NN
(
Wen et al., 2024
)
, which integrates persistence-image topology, graph convolutions, and tensor low-rank transformation layers. To obtain tensor-valued features, we truncate the encoder before vectorization and replace its final vector attention module with a lightweight multiway attention layer, yielding features of shape
(2H\times H\times H)
. These tensor features are fed into the FlowTGMM + CP-TDA head, and the encoder and flow are trained end-to-end. We set
H=32
, producing tensor features of dimension
64\times 32\times 32
. Training details are provided in Appendix
B
. All results follow the evaluation protocol of TTG-NN and
Xu et al., 2018
, using 10-fold cross-validation.

Performance relative to neural network baselines.

Table
4
compares STDN against 14 state-of-the-art graph neural network classifiers; additional kernel method comparisons are in Appendix
B
. STDN achieves the highest accuracy on both D&D and PROTEINS, outperforming all baselines including recent state-of-the-art models. These gains arise because deep neural networks typically employ dense linear classifier heads that do not exploit multiway structure, suffer from curse of dimensionality (akin to nonparametric regression) in small samples, and lack statistical guarantees. In contrast, our CP-TDA head imposes a parsimonious multilinear discriminant model, yields an optimal decision rule, and provides a theoretically grounded regularizations. The empirical improvements confirm that combining deep tensor representations with statistically principled discriminant analysis is materially more effective than purely neural network classifiers.

Performance relative to statistical tensor classifiers.

To compare directly against statistical tensor classifiers, we extract tensor features from graphs via the trained encoder-flow pipeline and evaluate CATCH, DATED, GuoSTM, LaiSTDA, TuckerLDA, and ZhouLogitCP under identical 10-fold cross-validation. Table
5
shows STDN achieves the best performance on both datasets. Competing methods rely on sparsity or Tucker structures, or lack direct discriminant modeling, failing to capture the most informative multilinear discriminant directions in the encoded features. By constraining the discriminant tensor to CP low-rank form, STDN recovers the dominant components of the decision boundary more accurately, yielding substantially lower misclassification error.

Additionally, the learned tensor representations exhibit numerically low multilinear rank. While not conclusive, this empirical pattern suggests that CP low-rank discriminant structure may be present in real data, providing practical justification for our modeling assumptions.

Across neural network and statistical baselines, STDN consistently achieves the best predictions. These results demonstrate that statistical discriminant structure and deep representation learning are mutually reinforcing: the encoder-flow pipeline provides rich tensor features, and CP-TDA extracts essential low-rank discriminant directions with theoretical guarantees and superior empirical accuracy.

7  Conclusion

This paper develops a new framework for high-dimensional tensor discriminant analysis that addresses key limitations of existing tensor classification approaches. We introduce CP low-rank structure for the discriminant tensor, a modeling perspective not previously explored, and develop CP-TDA algorithms with
rc-PCA
initialization that is essential for entering the global contraction region. Unlike prior CP methods,
rc-PCA
remains stable under weaker signal strength and with non-orthogonal CP bases, enabling global convergence of DISTIP-CP in regimes where standard initialization fails. Our theoretical analysis provides sharp perturbation bounds under correlated noise and establishes the first minimax-optimal misclassification rates for tensor discriminant analysis.

Beyond the correctly specified TGMM setting, we develop a semiparametric extension using learned tensor representations (STDN) and show, both theoretically and empirically, that CP low-rank discriminant analysis performs strongly even when the true model deviates from CP low-rankness. These results collectively demonstrate that statistical structure (here, CP-based discriminant modeling) can complement and strengthen modern representation learning, yielding interpretable, sample efficient, and robust tensor classifiers. Future work includes extending these ideas to broader tensor learning tasks, such as tensor regression and tensor clustering.

Data availability statement

The data that support the findings of this study are openly available in TUDataset at
https://chrsmrrs.github.io/datasets/
.

Supplementary Material of “High-Dimensional Tensor Discriminant Analysis: Low-Rank Discriminant Structure, Representation Synergy, and Theoretical Guarantees”

with dimensions

, but use orthogonal CP bases instead of non-orthogonal ones. For order-4 tensors, we set dimensions

, and CP rank
R=5
, using identity covariance matrices
\Sigma_{m}
for
m\in[4]
. Signal strengths follow two regimes: (i) small spectral separation with equal signal strength
w_{r}\in\{2.5,3.0,3.5\}
; and (ii) large spectral separation where
w_{r}/w_{r+1}=1.25
with
w_{\max}\in\{3,4,5\}
.

Implementation details.

We compare CP-TDA with several established tensor-based classifiers.
As a baseline without structural constraints, we include the sample discriminant tensor obtained from the plug-in estimator

\widehat{{\cal B}}=(\overline{{\cal X}}^{(2)}-\overline{{\cal X}}^{(1)})\times_{m=1}^{M}\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}

in equation (
4
). TuckerLDA applies Tucker decomposition

{\cal B}=\mathcal{F}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{M}\boldsymbol{U}_{M}
to the sample discriminant tensor with Tucker ranks
(5,5,5)
for order-3 tensors and
(5,5,5,5)
for order-4 tensors.

We evaluate CATCH (Covariate-Adjusted Tensor Classification in High-dimensions;
Pan et al., 2019
), implemented via the authors’ publicly available R package; DATED
(
Wang et al., 2024
)
, which uses tensor envelope structure for dimensionality reduction with mode-wise dimensions
(5,5,5)
for order-3 tensors and
(5,5,5,5)
for order-4 tensors; GuoSTM
(
Guo et al., 2016
)
, a support tensor machine using multilinear principal component analysis (MPCA) with output dimensions
(5,5,5)
for order-3 tensors and
(5,5,5,5)
for order-4 tensors; and LaiSTDA
(
Lai et al., 2013
)
, which imposes sparsity on discriminant directions with the same mode-wise dimensions. We also compare with ZhouLogitCP
(
Zhou et al., 2013
)
, a tensor generalized linear model that fits logistic regression with CP low-rank coefficients, fitted with ridge penalties
\rho\in\{0.001,0.005,0.01,0.05,0.1,0.5\}
at CP rank
R=5
and selected via BIC.

(a)

Small spectral separation and orthogonal bases

(b)

Large spectral separation and orthogonal bases

(c)

Small spectral separation and non-orthogonal bases

(d)

Large spectral separation and non-orthogonal bases

Figure 3
:
Logarithmic CP basis estimation error for
rc-PCA
and ARLS with orthogonal and non-orthogonal bases on order-4 tensors under (a,c) small spectral separation (equal
w_{r}
) and (b,d) large spectral separation (geometric decay with
w_{r}/w_{r+1}=1.25
).

Results.
Figures
2
-
3
and Tables
7
-
8
present results across different tensor orders and basis orthogonality conditions.
rc-PCA
consistently provides reliable initialization across all settings, while ARLS fails under weak signal conditions (Figures
2
-
3
). Performance comparisons show that CP-TDA achieves substantially lower estimation and classification errors than competing methods across all configurations (Tables
7
-
8
). Results for order-3 tensors with orthogonal bases and order-4 tensors with both orthogonal and non-orthogonal bases are consistent with the order-3 non-orthogonal setting in Section
5
, confirming CP-TDA’s robustness across tensor orders and basis types.
