\forall\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}},\;\theta_{\boldsymbol{v}}\in{\cal H}_{0},\;\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}}\neq\theta_{\boldsymbol{v}},
we need to verify two conditions:

(i)

the upper bound on the Kullback-Leibler divergence between
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{v}}}
, and

(ii)

the lower bound of
L_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})+L_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{v}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})
for
\boldsymbol{u}\neq\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}=0,\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}=0
.

We calculate the Kullback-Leibler divergence first. For
\boldsymbol{f}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{R}
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}=0
, define

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{\boldsymbol{u}}={\cal F}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times_{m=1}^{M}\boldsymbol{A}_{m},\;\vect({\cal F}_{\boldsymbol{u}})=\epsilon\boldsymbol{f}_{\boldsymbol{u}}+\lambda\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\;\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}}=\big({\cal M}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\;-{\cal M}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\;\boldsymbol{I}_{d}\big)\in{\cal H}_{0}.

and consider the distribution
{\cal T}{\cal N}({\cal M}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\;\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
.
Then the Kullback-Leibler divergence between
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{v}}}
can be bounded by

\displaystyle{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}}},\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{v}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\vect({\cal M}_{\boldsymbol{u}})-\vect({\cal M}_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\vect({\cal F}_{\boldsymbol{u}})-\vect({\cal F}_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{c^{2}R}{2n}.

In addition, by applying Lemma
G.11
, we have that for any
\boldsymbol{f}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{f}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\{0,1\}^{R}
,

\displaystyle L_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{u}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})+L_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{v}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\Delta}e^{-\Delta^{2}/8}\cdot\left\lVert\vect({\cal F}_{\boldsymbol{u}})-\vect({\cal F}_{\boldsymbol{v}})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\Delta}e^{-\Delta^{2}/8}\sqrt{\frac{R}{8}\cdot\frac{c^{2}}{n}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{1}{\Delta}e^{-\Delta^{2}/8}\sqrt{\frac{R}{n}}.

So far we have verified the aforementioned conditions (i) and (ii). Lemma
G.12
immediately implies that, there exists some constant
C_{\gamma}>0
, such that

\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\theta\in{\cal H}_{0}}\mathbb{P}\left(L_{\theta}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})\geq C_{\gamma}\frac{1}{\Delta}e^{-\Delta^{2}/8}\sqrt{\frac{R}{n}}\right)\geq 1-\gamma

(66)

Combining (
66
) and (
65
), we have

\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\theta\in{\cal H}_{0}}\mathbb{P}\left({\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\geq C_{\gamma}\frac{1}{\Delta}e^{-\Delta^{2}/8}\cdot\frac{R}{n}\right)\geq 1-\gamma.

(67)

Similarly, for each
{\cal H}_{m}
,
m=1,...,M
, we can obtain that, there exists some constant
C_{\gamma}>0
, such that

\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\theta\in{\cal H}_{m}}\mathbb{P}\left({\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\geq C_{\gamma}\frac{1}{\Delta}e^{-\Delta^{2}/8}\cdot\frac{d_{m}R}{n}\right)\geq 1-\gamma.

(68)

Finally combining (
67
) and (
68
), we obtain the desired lower bound for the excess misclassficiation error

\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\theta\in{\cal H}}\mathbb{P}\left({\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\geq C_{\gamma}\frac{1}{\Delta}e^{-\Delta^{2}/8}\cdot\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R+R}{n}\right)\geq 1-\gamma.

(69)

This implies that if
c_{1}<\Delta\leq c_{2}
for some
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\theta\in{\cal H}}\mathbb{P}\left({\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\geq C_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R}{n}\right)\geq 1-\gamma.

On the other hand, if
\Delta\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, then for any
\vartheta>0

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp}}\sup_{\theta\in{\cal H}}\mathbb{P}\left({\cal R}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm cp})-{\cal R}_{\rm opt}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\geq C_{\gamma}\exp\left\{-\left(\frac{1}{8}+\vartheta\right)\Delta^{2}\right\}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}R}{n}\right)\geq 1-\gamma.

Appendix F
Proofs for Section
4

F.1
Proof of Proposition
4.2

We derive the log-likelihood in (
22
) and establish the corresponding population objective. By convention, maximum likelihood estimation minimizes the negative log-likelihood (the loss function).

Sample log-likelihood.

Given a labeled dataset
\{(\mathcal{X}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{0}}
where
\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
are tensor-valued observations and
Y_{i}\in[K]
are class labels, the sample log-likelihood is

\mathcal{L}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}[\log\pi_{Y_{i}}+\log p_{\mathcal{X}|Y}(\mathcal{X}_{i}\mid Y_{i})]
,
where
\pi_{k}
are the class priors and
p_{\mathcal{X}|Y}(\cdot\mid k)
is the class-conditional density. Since the prior terms
\log\pi_{Y_{i}}
do not depend on the flow parameters
\varphi
or the dataset
\{(\mathcal{X}_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{0}}
, they can be omitted during optimization.

Fix an observation
i
and denote
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}=\mathcal{X}_{\beta}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})
as the encoder output. The Tensor RealNVP flow applies
L
transformations sequentially: for
\ell=1,\ldots,L
, define
\mathcal{X}^{(\ell)}=g^{(\ell)}(\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)})
, where each
g^{(\ell)}
consists of a mode-wise linear mixer followed by an affine coupling, as specified in Definition
4.1
. Since the mode-wise mixer
\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}}
are invertible matrices, and the affine coupling uses elementwise exponentiation, each block
g^{(\ell)}
is a bijection. Therefore, the full flow
g_{\varphi}=g^{(L)}\circ\cdots\circ g^{(1)}
is a composition of bijections, with Jacobian given by the chain rule:

\mathbf{J}_{g_{\varphi}}(\mathcal{X}^{(0)})=\mathbf{J}_{g^{(L)}}(\mathcal{X}^{(L-1)})\cdots\mathbf{J}_{g^{(2)}}(\mathcal{X}^{(1)})\mathbf{J}_{g^{(1)}}(\mathcal{X}^{(0)}).

Let
f(\cdot\mid Y=k;\mathcal{M}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
denote the tensor normal density in the latent space, where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
represents the mode-wise covariances. By the change of variables formula for probability densities:

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{X}|Y}(\mathcal{X}^{(0)}\mid k)

\displaystyle=f(g_{\varphi}(\mathcal{X}^{(0)})\mid Y=k;\mathcal{M}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{g_{\varphi}}(\mathcal{X}^{(0)})\right|

\displaystyle=f(\mathcal{X}^{(L)}\mid Y=k;\mathcal{M}_{k},\Sigma)\prod_{\ell=1}^{L}\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}(\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)})\right|,

where we used the multiplicative property of determinants:
\det(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B})=\det(\mathbf{A})\det(\mathbf{B})
.
Taking logarithms and summing over all observations
i=1,\ldots,n_{0}
yields the sample loss:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi)=-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\left[\log f(\mathcal{X}_{i}^{(L)}\mid Y=Y_{i};\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{Y_{i}},\widehat{\Sigma})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}(\mathcal{X}_{i}^{(\ell-1)})\right|\right],

where
\mathcal{X}_{i}^{(\ell)}=g^{(\ell)}(\mathcal{X}_{i}^{(\ell-1)})
for
\ell=1,\ldots,L
with
\mathcal{X}_{i}^{(0)}=\mathcal{X}_{\beta}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{k},\widehat{\Sigma}
are computed from the latent features
\{\mathcal{X}_{i}^{(L)}\}_{i=1}^{n_{0}}
. This establishes (
22
).

Population loss.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{\beta,k}
denote the unknown distribution of encoder features
\mathcal{X}_{\beta}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})
conditioned on label
k
with prior
\pi_{k}
. The population loss is obtained by replacing the empirical average with expectation and using population parameters:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi)=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\beta,k}}\left[\log f(g_{\varphi}(\mathcal{X})\mid Y=k;\mathcal{M}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}(\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)})\right|\right],

(70)

where
\mathcal{M}_{k}=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}^{(L)}\mid Y=k]
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are the population class means and mode-wise covariances.

Log-Jacobian of a Tensor RealNVP block.

We now derive the log-Jacobian formula (
23
) for a single flow block
g^{(\ell)}
. We suppress the superscript
\ell
for notational clarity. By Definition
4.1
, each block
g
consists of two components applied sequentially: (i) a mode-wise linear mixer, and (ii) an affine coupling based on a binary mask. The mixer transforms
\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{(\ell)}:=\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}\times_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}
, where each
\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}}
is an invertible mode-mixer matrix. Under vectorization, this multilinear operation becomes

\text{vec}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{(\ell)})=(\mathbf{H}_{M}^{(\ell)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{H}_{1}^{(\ell)})\text{vec}(\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}),

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product. Therefore, the Jacobian of the mixer is

\mathbf{J}_{\text{mix}}=\frac{\partial\;\text{vec}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{(\ell)})}{\partial\;\text{vec}(\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)})}=\mathbf{H}_{M}^{(\ell)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{H}_{1}^{(\ell)}.

Using the Kronecker product determinant formula, we obtain
\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{mix}}=\prod_{m=1}^{M}(\det\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)})^{d/d_{m}}
, where
d=\prod_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}
. Since each
\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}
is orthogonal, taking logarithms yields

\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{mix}}\right|=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{d}{d_{m}}\log\left|\det\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}\right|=0.

Next, we analyze the affine coupling transformation. By Definition
4.1
, the affine coupling uses a pre-specified binary mask
\mathcal{K}^{(\ell)}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
to partition the indices into two disjoint sets:

\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}=\{\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}):\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{i}}^{(\ell)}=1\},\quad\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}=\{\mathbf{i}:\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{i}}^{(\ell)}=0\}.

The coupling transformation is defined as

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)},\quad\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\odot\exp\left(v^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right)\right)+t^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right),

where
v^{(\ell)},t^{(\ell)}
are tensor-valued conditioner networks,
\odot
denotes elementwise multiplication, and
\exp(\cdot)
is applied elementwise. Ordering coordinates as
(\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)},\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)})
in both input and output, the Jacobian matrix has a block lower-triangular structure:

\mathbf{J}_{\text{cpl}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{|\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}|}&0\\[5.0pt]
*&\text{Diag}\left(\exp\left(v^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right)\right)\right)\end{bmatrix},

where the upper-left block is identity since
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}
; the upper-right block is zero since
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}
does not depend on
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}
; the lower-right diagonal block comes from the elementwise scaling
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\odot\exp(v^{(\ell)}(\cdot))
; and the lower-left block (
*
) contains derivatives through both
v^{(\ell)}
and
t^{(\ell)}
, which do not affect the determinant. For a block-triangular matrix, the determinant is the product of diagonal block determinants:

\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{cpl}}=\prod_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}\exp\left(v^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right)_{\mathbf{j}}\right)=\exp\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}v^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right)_{\mathbf{j}}\right).

Taking logarithms:
\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{cpl}}\right|=\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}v^{(\ell)}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\right)_{\mathbf{j}}
. Since
g^{(\ell)}=
(affine coupling)
\circ
(mode-wise mixer), by the chain rule:

\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}(\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)})\right|=\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{cpl}}\right|+\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{mix}}\right|.

Plugging in the expressions for
\log|\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{mix}}|
and
\log|\det\mathbf{J}_{\text{cpl}}|
establishes the log-Jacobian formula (
23
).

F.2
Proof of Theorem
4.3

We proceed in three steps: distributional mismatch, CP structural bias under the surrogate distribution, and finite
-
sample estimation of the oracle CP rank-
R
discriminant under the surrogate.

Step 1. Distributional mismatch.

We first define the weighted total variation distance. For two probability measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
with densities
p
and
q
with respect to a common dominating measure, the total variation distance is

\text{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\frac{1}{2}\int|p(z)-q(z)|\,dz=\frac{1}{2}\|p-q\|_{1}.

We define the weighted total variation between the true latent distributions and their TGMM surrogates as

\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}:=\pi_{1}\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1})+\pi_{2}\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}).

For a classifier
\Upsilon:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}\to\{1,2\}
with decision region
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\{\mathcal{X}:\Upsilon(\mathcal{X})=1\}
, the misclassification risk under
\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
is

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon)=\pi_{1}\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})+\pi_{2}\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}(\mathcal{R}_{1}),

where
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
is the complement. The Bayes-optimal risk is
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}=\inf_{\Upsilon}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon)
. Similarly, we define
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon)
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}
under the surrogate distribution. We establish two key properties relating risks under different distributions:

Claim 1.
For any fixed classifier
\Upsilon
,
|\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon)|\leq\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
.

Claim 2.
For the Bayes-optimal risks,
|\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}|\leq\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
.

Using these claims, for the estimated classifier
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
, we decompose the excess risk:

\displaystyle\small\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})\right)+\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\right)+\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\right)+2\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}.

(71)

Proof of Claim 1.
Starting from the definition and splitting by classes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon)-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})\right)+\pi_{2}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}(\mathcal{R}_{1})-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}(\mathcal{R}_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}|\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1}(\mathcal{R}_{2})|+\pi_{2}|\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}(\mathcal{R}_{1})-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}(\mathcal{R}_{1})|.

(72)

Fix
k\in\{1,2\}
. Let
\mu_{k}=\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}+\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
be a dominating measure and denote
p_{k}=\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}}{d\mu_{k}}
,
q_{k}=\frac{d\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}}{d\mu_{k}}
as the Radon-Nikodym derivatives. For any measurable set
B
,

\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(B)-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(B)=\int_{B}(p_{k}-q_{k})\,d\mu_{k}.

By the triangle inequality:

|\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(B)-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(B)|\leq\int_{B}|p_{k}-q_{k}|\,d\mu_{k}\leq\int|p_{k}-q_{k}|\,d\mu_{k}=2\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}).

Since
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are complementary,
|\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{R}_{1})-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{R}_{1})|=|\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{R}_{2})-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{R}_{2})|
. Therefore,

|\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{R}_{1})-\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{R}_{1})|\leq\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}).

Substituting into (
F.2
) establishes Claim 1.

Proof of Claim 2.
Let
p_{k}
and
q_{k}
denote the densities of
\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
with respect to a common dominating measure
\mu
. For any decision region
A
,

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(A)=\int\left[\mathbf{1}_{A}(z)\pi_{1}p_{1}(z)+\mathbf{1}_{A^{c}}(z)\pi_{2}p_{2}(z)\right]d\mu(z).

The Bayes-optimal classifier chooses
A^{*}=\{z:\pi_{1}p_{1}(z)\geq\pi_{2}p_{2}(z)\}
, yielding

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}=\int\min\{\pi_{1}p_{1}(z),\pi_{2}p_{2}(z)\}\,d\mu(z).

Using the identity
\min\{u,v\}=\frac{1}{2}(u+v-|u-v|)
and noting that
\int\pi_{k}p_{k}\,d\mu=\pi_{k}
:

\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\|\pi_{1}p_{1}-\pi_{2}p_{2}\|_{1}\right).

Similarly,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\|\pi_{1}q_{1}-\pi_{2}q_{2}\|_{1}\right)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left|\|\pi_{1}p_{1}-\pi_{2}p_{2}\|_{1}-\|\pi_{1}q_{1}-\pi_{2}q_{2}\|_{1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|(\pi_{1}p_{1}-\pi_{2}p_{2})-(\pi_{1}q_{1}-\pi_{2}q_{2})\|_{1}\quad\text{(reverse triangle inequality)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\pi_{1}(p_{1}-q_{1})-\pi_{2}(p_{2}-q_{2})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\pi_{1}\|p_{1}-q_{1}\|_{1}+\pi_{2}\|p_{2}-q_{2}\|_{1}\right)\quad\text{(triangle inequality)}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1})+\pi_{2}\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2})=\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}.

This establishes Claim 2 and completes the proof of inequality (
F.2
).

Bounding the total variation distance.
The preceding arguments establish inequality (
F.2
), which decomposes the excess risk into two components: (i) the excess risk of
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
under the surrogate TGMM distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
, and (ii) the distributional mismatch term
2\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
. We now bound the total variation distance by relating it to the FlowTGMM training objectives.

Applying Pinsker’s inequality class-wise and then the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields:

\displaystyle\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1})+\pi_{2}\text{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2},\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2})

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{1}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1})}+\pi_{2}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2})}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\pi_{1}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},1})+\pi_{2}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},2})\right)},

where
\text{KL}(\cdot\|\cdot)
denotes the Kullback-Leibler divergence. Thus, controlling the weighted KL sum between the class-conditional latent distributions and their TGMM surrogates suffices to bound the total variation.

Let
p_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
and
q_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
denote the densities of
\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
, respectively, where
\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}
is the distribution of latent features
\mathcal{X}^{(L)}=g_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\mathcal{X}_{\beta}(\mathcal{Z}))
for class
k
. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\beta,k}
denote the distribution of encoder outputs
\mathcal{X}_{\beta}(\mathcal{Z})
before applying the flow. By the change of variables formula and the invertibility of
g_{\varphi}
:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k})

\displaystyle=\int p_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{X}^{(L)})\log\frac{p_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{X}^{(L)})}{q_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(\mathcal{X}^{(L)})}\,d\mathcal{X}^{(L)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\beta,k}}\left[\log p_{\beta,k}(\mathcal{X})-\log\left|\det\mathbf{J}_{g_{\widehat{\varphi}}}(\mathcal{X})\right|-\log q_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}(g_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\mathcal{X}))\right],

where
p_{\beta,k}
is the density of the encoder features before the flow transformation. Weighting by class priors and summing over
k\in\{1,2\}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2}\pi_{k}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k})=\underbrace{-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\pi_{k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\beta,k}}\left[\log f(g_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\mathcal{X})\mid Y=k;\mathcal{M}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})+\log|\det\mathbf{J}_{g_{\widehat{\varphi}}}(\mathcal{X})|\right]}_{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})}-C_{\beta},

(73)

where
f(\cdot\mid Y=k;\mathcal{M}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
denotes the tensor normal density and
C_{\beta}=-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\pi_{k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\beta,k}}[\log p_{\beta,k}(\mathcal{X})]
is a constant independent of
\widehat{\varphi}
,
\mathcal{M}_{k}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. The underbraced term is precisely the population FlowTGMM loss from (
70
).

By Definition
4.1
, the Tensor RealNVP
g_{\varphi}=g^{(L)}\circ\cdots\circ g^{(1)}
is a composition of bijective transformations (mode-wise linear mixers and affine couplings), each of which is
C^{2}
-smooth with
C^{2}
-smooth inverse. Therefore,
g_{\varphi}
is a
C^{2}
-diffeomorphism. By Theorem 1 of
Teshima et al., 2020
, coupling-based normalizing flows are
L^{p}
-universal approximators for
C^{2}
-diffeomorphisms. Furthermore, by Corollary 1 of
Teshima et al., 2020
(see also Theorem 42 and Corollary 41 of
Ishikawa et al., 2023
), this
L^{p}
-universality implies distributional universality: there exist flow parameters
\varphi^{\prime}
such that the transformed distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\varphi^{\prime},k}
is arbitrarily close to its moment-matched TGMM surrogate
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\varphi^{\prime},k}
in KL divergence. This implies

\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\varphi^{\prime},k}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\varphi^{\prime},k})=0.

Consequently, from (
73
),

\sum_{k=1}^{2}\pi_{k}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi},k})=\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime}),

since the constant
C_{\beta}
cancels when taking the difference. Combining with Pinsker’s inequality, the total variation can be bounded by
\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})\right)}
. By Lemma
F.2
under Assumption
F.1
,
with probability at least
1-n_{0}^{-1}
,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})\;\lesssim\;\Big(\tfrac{\log n_{0}}{n_{0}}\Big)^{1/2}
, and hence
\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}\;\lesssim\;\Big(\tfrac{\log n_{0}}{n_{0}}\Big)^{1/4}
.

Step 2. CP structural bias under the latent TGMM.
For any tensor
{\cal B}^{\prime}
, consider the linear score

s_{{\cal B}^{\prime}}({\cal X}):=\langle{\cal X}-{\cal M},{\cal B}^{\prime}\rangle+\eta,\qquad\eta:=\log(\pi_{1}/\pi_{0}),

where
{\cal M}=({\cal M}_{0}+{\cal M}_{1})/2
. Under the TGMM surrogate
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
, the distribution of
s_{{\cal B}^{\prime}}({\cal X})\mid Y=k
is Gaussian with

\mathbb{E}[s_{{\cal B}^{\prime}}({\cal X})\mid Y=0]=\eta-\frac{1}{2}\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}^{\prime}\rangle,\qquad\mathbb{E}[s_{{\cal B}^{\prime}}({\cal X})\mid Y=1]=\eta+\frac{1}{2}\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}^{\prime}\rangle,

and common variance
v({\cal B}^{\prime}):=\|{\cal B}^{\prime}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{-1/2}\|_{F}^{2}\equiv\|\gamma^{\prime}\|_{F}^{2},\;\gamma^{\prime}:={\cal B}^{\prime}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{-1/2}
.
Therefore, with
s({\cal B}^{\prime})=\sqrt{v({\cal B}^{\prime})}>0
,

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B}^{\prime})=\pi_{0}\phi\left(\frac{\eta}{s({\cal B}^{\prime})}-\frac{\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}^{\prime}\rangle}{2\,s({\cal B}^{\prime})}\right)+\pi_{1}\phi\left(-\frac{\eta}{s({\cal B}^{\prime})}-\frac{\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}^{\prime}\rangle}{2\,s({\cal B}^{\prime})}\right).

For the Fisher discriminant tensor
{\cal B}:={\cal D}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{-1}
,
\Delta:=\sqrt{\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}\rangle}=\|{\cal B}\times_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m}^{-1/2}\|_{F}=s({\cal B})
it reduces to

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B})=\pi_{0}\phi\left(\frac{\eta}{\Delta}-\frac{\Delta}{2}\right)+\pi_{1}\phi\left(-\frac{\eta}{\Delta}-\frac{\Delta}{2}\right),

where
\phi
is the CDF of the standard normal. To isolate the structural CP bias, we compare the Bayes rule
{\cal B}
to the best CP rank-
R
discriminant at the same score scale:

\mathcal{B}_{R}\in\arg\max_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\in\mathcal{C}_{R}}\langle\mathcal{D},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{R}\times_{m=1}^{M}(\Sigma_{m})^{1/2}\right\|_{F}=\left\|\mathcal{B}\times_{m}(\Sigma_{m})^{1/2}\right\|_{F},

where
\mathcal{C}_{R}
denotes the cone of CP rank-
R
tensors yielding
\mathrm{SNR}

\Delta_{R}=\sqrt{\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{B}_{R}\rangle}
and efficiency loss

\delta_{\mathrm{cp}}:=\Delta-\Delta_{R}\geq 0
. This fixing
s({\cal B}_{R})=\Delta
makes the variance of
s_{{\cal B}_{R}}({\cal X})
match that of
s_{{\cal B}}({\cal X})
and converts the risk difference into a function of a single margin parameter:
\mu({\cal B}^{\prime}):=\frac{\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}^{\prime}\rangle}{2\,s({\cal B}^{\prime})}
. Write

\mu({\cal B})=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{2\Delta}=\frac{\Delta}{2},\qquad\mu({\cal B}_{R,*})=\frac{\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}_{R}\rangle}{2\Delta}=\frac{\Delta_{R}^{2}}{2\Delta},

where
\Delta_{R}:=\sqrt{\langle{\cal D},{\cal B}_{R}\rangle}\leq\Delta
. Recall
\delta_{\mathrm{cp}}:=\Delta-\Delta_{R}\geq 0
. Then the difference is

\mu({\cal B})-\mu({\cal B}_{R})=\frac{\Delta^{2}-\Delta_{R}^{2}}{2\Delta}=\frac{(\Delta-\Delta_{R})(\Delta+\Delta_{R})}{2\Delta}\in\left[\frac{\delta_{\text{CP}}}{2},\delta_{\text{CP}}\right],

(74)

since
0\leq\Delta_{R}\leq\Delta
. Fix
\Delta>0
and
\eta=\log(\pi_{1}/\pi_{0})
. With the scale constraint
s({\cal B}_{R})=\Delta
, define

f(\mu):=\pi_{0}\phi(a-\mu)+\pi_{1}\phi(-a-\mu),\quad a:=\frac{\eta}{\Delta}.

Then
{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B})=f(\mu({\cal B}))=f\left(\frac{\Delta}{2}\right),\;{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B}_{R})=f(\mu({\cal B}_{R}))
. Using
\phi^{\prime}(t)=\phi(t)
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)=-t\phi(t)
,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\pi_{0}\phi(a-\mu)-\pi_{1}\phi(-a-\mu)<0,

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\pi_{0}(a-\mu)\phi(a-\mu)-\pi_{1}(-a-\mu)\phi(-a-\mu).

Set
\mu_{*}:=\mu({\cal B})=\Delta/2
and
\mu_{R}:=\mu({\cal B}_{R})\leq\mu_{*}
. By Taylor’s theorem,

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B}_{R})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B})=f(\mu_{R})-f(\mu_{*})=f^{\prime}(\mu_{*})(\mu_{R}-\mu_{*})+\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi)(\mu_{R}-\mu_{*})^{2}

(75)

for some
\xi
between
\mu_{R}
and
\mu_{*}
. Since
f^{\prime}(\mu_{*})<0
and
\mu_{R}-\mu_{*}\leq 0
, the leading term is nonnegative. We now bound the two terms in (
75
) in two regimes:

When
\Delta=O(1)
,
\phi(a-\mu)
and
\phi(-a-\mu)
are bounded away from
\pm\infty
. Using
\phi(t)\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}
and
|a|=|\eta/\Delta|\lesssim_{\pi_{0},\pi_{1}}1
for fixed priors,
|f^{\prime}(\mu_{*})|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\;|f^{\prime\prime}(\xi)|\leq C
for some universal constant
C>0
. We have, from (
75
),

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B}_{R})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B})\leq C_{1}|\mu_{*}-\mu_{R}|+C_{2}|\mu_{*}-\mu_{R}|^{2}\lesssim\delta_{\text{CP}}+\delta_{\text{CP}}^{2}\lesssim\delta_{\text{CP}},

since
\Delta
is bounded.

When
\Delta
is large (unbounded), by the fundamental calculus,

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B}_{R})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B})=f(\mu_{R})-f(\mu_{*})=\int_{\mu_{R}}^{\mu_{*}}(-f^{\prime}(\mu))d\mu.

For
\mu_{R}\geq|a|
under the large margin regime, both coefficients in
f^{\prime\prime}(\mu)
are non-positive, hence
-f^{\prime}
is decreasing on
[\mu_{R},\mu_{*}]
, so

\int_{\mu_{R}}^{\mu_{*}}(-f^{\prime}(\mu))d\mu\leq(\mu_{*}-\mu_{R})(-f^{\prime}(\mu_{R})).

Using
|a-\mu_{R}|\geq\mu_{R}-|a|
,
|-a-\mu_{R}|=\mu_{R}+|a|\geq\mu_{R}-|a|
and
\phi
is decreasing on
[0,\infty)
,

-f^{\prime}(\mu_{R})=\pi_{0}\phi(a-\mu_{R})+\pi_{1}\phi(-a-\mu_{R})\leq(\pi_{0}+\pi_{1})\phi(\mu_{R}-|a|)=\phi(\mu_{R}-|a|).

Combining the above two inequalities and
\phi(u)\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-u^{2}/2}
yields

\displaystyle{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B}_{R})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B})\leq(\mu_{*}-\mu_{R})\phi(\mu_{R}-|a|)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(\mu_{*}-\mu_{R})\exp\left(-\frac{(\mu_{R}-|a|)^{2}}{2}\right)

(76)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\delta_{\mathrm{cp}}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{(\Delta_{R}^{2}-2|\eta|)^{2}}{8\Delta^{2}}\right).

The bound depends on
\Delta_{R}
through
\mu_{R}=\Delta_{R}^{2}/(2\Delta)
. Denote
\varepsilon_{R}:=\delta_{\mathrm{cp}}/\Delta\in[0,1]
. Since
|\eta|=|\log(\pi_{1}/\pi_{0})|
is an
O(1)
constant,

\frac{(\Delta_{R}^{2}-2|\eta|)^{2}}{8\Delta^{2}}=\frac{(1-\varepsilon_{R})^{4}}{8}\Delta^{2}+O(1),

if
\varepsilon_{R}
is bounded away from 1, which happens for reasonable oracle CP approximation. Furthermore,
\varepsilon_{R}\to 0
as
\Delta\to\infty
gives

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B}_{R})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\cal B})\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\delta_{\mathrm{cp}}\cdot\exp\left(-\left(\frac{1}{8}+o(1)\right)\Delta^{2}\right).

To summarize, under
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
and the scale match
s({\cal B}_{R})=\Delta
,

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon_{B_{R}})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\lesssim\begin{cases}\delta_{\mathrm{cp}},&(\Delta=O(1)),\\
\delta_{\mathrm{cp}}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{(1-\varepsilon_{R})^{4}}{8}\Delta^{2}+O(1)\right),&(0<\varepsilon_{R}<1,\Delta\to\infty).\end{cases}

Step 3. Statistical error of the oracle CP rank-R discriminant under the latent TGMM.

Theorem
3.3
applies to the TGMM surrogate
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}
with shared mode-wise covariances and a CP rank-
R
discriminant head. The discriminant is computed using the procedure from Section
2.2
by replacing
{\cal X}_{i}
with
{\cal X}_{i}^{(L)}
, therefore we may invoke Theorem
3.3
with
\Delta
replaced by
\Delta_{R}
. Concretely, under the conditions of Theorem
3.1
and the signal condition in Theorem
3.3
, we have with high probability:

{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-{\cal R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon_{B_{R}})\lesssim\begin{cases}\frac{\sum_{m}d_{m}R}{n},&\Delta_{R}=O(1),\\
e^{-\left(\frac{1}{8}+o(1)\right)\Delta_{R}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\sum_{m}d_{m}R}{n},&\Delta_{R}\to\infty\;\text{as}\;n\to\infty.\end{cases}

(77)

Combining the three steps.
We decompose the excess risk as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}=\left[\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})\right]+\left[\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{B}_{R}})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left[\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{B}_{R}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\right]+\left[\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{2\text{TV}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}_{\text{Step 1: distributional mismatch}}+\underbrace{\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{B}_{R}})\right)}_{\text{Step 3: statistical estimation error}}+\underbrace{\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{B}_{R}})-\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\varphi}}}^{*}\right)}_{\text{Step 2: CP structural bias}}.

Substituting the bounds from Steps 1-3 completes the proof.

To formalize the approximation and generalization properties of FlowTGMM, we introduce the following regularity assumption and generalization bound.

Assumption F.1

(Regularity conditions for FlowTGMM)
.

Consider the Tensor RealNVP flow
g_{\varphi}=g^{(L)}\circ\cdots\circ g^{(1)}
from Definition
4.1
with bounded input
\|\mathcal{X}^{(0)}\|_{F}\leq C_{0}
for some constant
C_{0}>0
. The conditioner networks
v^{(\ell)},t^{(\ell)}
for each block
g^{(\ell)}
,
\ell=1,\ldots,L
, are
L_{c}
-Lipschitz continuous and the scale network satisfies
\|v^{(\ell)}(\mathcal{X})\|_{1}\leq C_{v}
for all
\mathcal{X}
, where
L_{c},C_{v}>0
are constants.

The Lipschitz continuity assumption on both
v^{(\ell)}
and
t^{(\ell)}
is standard for networks with bounded weights and Lipschitz activation functions (e.g. ReLU,
\tanh
). The
\ell_{1}
bound on the scale network
v^{(\ell)}
is a common design choice in normalizing-flow architectures: the original RealNVP
(
Dinh et al., 2017
)
apply a
\tanh
activation followed by a learnable scaling factor, which naturally bounds the sum of scale values and prevents numerical instabilities from exponentially large Jacobian determinants.

Lemma F.2

(Generalization bound for FlowTGMM)
.

Under Assumption
F.1
, suppose the training algorithm produces parameters
\widehat{\varphi}
satisfying
\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\mathrm{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\mathrm{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n_{0}^{-1/2})
, where
\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\mathrm{Flow}}(\varphi)
is the empirical loss defined in (
22
) and the infimum is taken over all
\varphi^{\prime}
satisfying Assumption
F.1
. Then with probability at least
1-n_{0}^{-1}
,

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})\lesssim\big(\frac{\log n_{0}}{n_{0}}\big)^{1/2},

where
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi)
is the population loss defined in (
70
).

Proof.

We decompose the population loss gap into optimization and generalization errors:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left[\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})\right]+\left[\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle+\left[\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})\right],

(78)

where the first and third terms measure generalization gaps at
\widehat{\varphi}
and at the optimum, respectively, while the second term is the optimization error. Since both
\widehat{\varphi}
and the minimizers of the empirical and population losses lie in the parameter space
\Phi
satisfying Assumption
F.1
, the first and third terms are both bounded by the uniform generalization gap
\sup_{\varphi\in\Phi}|\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi)|
. By assumption, the optimization error is negligible. Therefore,

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\widehat{\varphi})-\inf_{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi^{\prime})\leq 2\sup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\left|\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi)\right|+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n_{0}^{-1/2}).

(79)

Let
\ell(\mathcal{X};\varphi)
denote the per-sample FlowTGMM loss, so that

\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\ell(\mathcal{X}_{i};\varphi),\quad\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi)=\mathbb{E}[\ell(\mathcal{X};\varphi)].

Define the loss class
\mathcal{F}:=\{\ell(\cdot;\varphi):\varphi\in\Phi\}
. We first establish that the per-sample loss is uniformly bounded. Under Assumption
F.1
, the input satisfies
\|\mathcal{X}^{(0)}\|_{F}\leq C_{0}
. Each Tensor RealNVP block
g^{(\ell)}
transforms
\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}
via a mode-wise orthogonal mixer followed by an affine coupling. Since
\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}
are orthogonal matrices, the mixer preserves the Frobenius norm:

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{(\ell)}\|_{F}=\|\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}\times_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}\|_{F}\leq\prod_{m=1}^{M}\|\mathbf{H}_{m}^{(\ell)}\|\cdot\|\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}\|_{F}=\|\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}\|_{F}.

For the affine coupling, by the
L_{c}
-Lipschitz assumption and the uniform boundedness,

\|\mathcal{X}^{(\ell)}\|_{F}\leq\|\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{(\ell)}\|_{F}\cdot e^{C_{v}}+L_{c}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\|_{F}\leq(e^{C_{v}}+L_{c})\|\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{(\ell)}\|_{F}\leq(e^{C_{v}}+L_{c})\|\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)}\|_{F}.

Combining these bounds across all
L
layers yields
\|\mathcal{X}^{(L)}\|_{F}\leq C_{0}(e^{C_{v}}+L_{c})^{L}=:C_{L}
.

For the tensor normal log-density, the only term that depends on
\mathcal{X}^{(L)}
is the quadratic form, as the normalizing constant does not affect the generalization gap. Since
\|\mathcal{X}^{(L)}\|_{F}\leq C_{L}
and the class means satisfy
\|\mathcal{M}_{k}\|_{F}=\|\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}^{(L)}\mid Y=k]\|_{F}\leq C_{L}
, we have
\|\mathcal{X}^{(L)}-\mathcal{M}_{k}\|_{F}\leq 2C_{L}
. Let
\lambda_{\min}>0
denote the smallest eigenvalue among all mode-wise covariances
\{\Sigma_{m}\}_{m=1}^{M}
. Under vectorization,

\left|\left\langle\text{vec}(\mathcal{X}^{(L)}-\mathcal{M}_{k}),\,(\Sigma_{M}^{-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sigma_{1}^{-1})\text{vec}(\mathcal{X}^{(L)}-\mathcal{M}_{k})\right\rangle\right|\;\leq\;\lambda_{\min}^{-M}\|\mathcal{X}^{(L)}-\mathcal{M}_{k}\|_{F}^{2}\;\leq\;4\lambda_{\min}^{-M}C_{L}^{2}.

For the log-Jacobian, by (
23
) and Assumption
F.1
,
\left|\log|\det\mathbf{J}_{g^{(\ell)}}(\mathcal{X}^{(\ell-1)})|\right|=\left|\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(\ell)}}v^{(\ell)}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)})_{\mathbf{j}}\right|\leq\|v^{(\ell)}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)})\|_{1}\leq C_{v}
. Summing over
\ell=1,\ldots,L
and combining with the log-density bound, there exists a finite constant
B>0
depending on
(C_{0},L_{c},C_{v},L,\lambda_{\min})
such that
|\ell(\mathcal{X};\varphi)|\leq B
for all
{\cal X}
and
\varphi\in\Phi
.

Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{0}}(\mathcal{F})
denote the empirical Rademacher complexity of
\mathcal{F}
. By standard Rademacher generalization bounds for bounded loss functions
(
Bartlett and Mendelson, 2002
;
Koltchinskii and Panchenko, 2002
)
, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\left|\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi)\right|\leq 2\,\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{0}}(\mathcal{F})+4B\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2/\delta)}{n_{0}}}.

(80)

We now bound
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{0}}(\mathcal{F})
using the structure of the FlowTGMM loss. Under Assumption
F.1
, the conditioner networks are Lipschitz continuous with bounded inputs, and the loss is bounded by constant
B
. Norm-based Rademacher complexity bounds for deep networks
(
Bartlett et al., 2017
)
establish that
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n_{0}}(\mathcal{F})\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}
. Substituting into (
80
) with
\delta=1/n_{0}
yields

\sup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\left|\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\text{Flow}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{n_{0},\text{Flow}}(\varphi)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}+4B\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2n_{0})}{n_{0}}}=O\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log n_{0}}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right)

with probability at least
1-1/n_{0}
. Combining with (
79
) completes the proof.
∎

Appendix G
Technical Lemmas

We collect all technical lemmas that has been used in the theoretical proofs throughout the paper in this section. Let
d=d_{1}d_{2}\cdots d_{M}
and
d_{-m}=d/d_{m}.
Denote
\|A\|_{2}
or
\|A\|
as the spectral norm of a matrix
A
. Also let
\otimes
be the Kronecker product, and
\circ
be the tensor outer product.

Lemma G.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times r}
and
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times r}
with

\|\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\|_{\rm 2}\vee\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\|_{\rm 2}\leq\delta
and
d_{1}\wedge d_{2}\geq r
.
Let
\boldsymbol{A}=\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{D}_{1}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}^{\top}
be the SVD of
\boldsymbol{A}
,

\boldsymbol{U}=\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{B}=\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{D}_{2}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}

the SVD of
\boldsymbol{B}
, and
\boldsymbol{V}=\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}
.
Then,
\|\boldsymbol{A}\Lambda\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{U}\Lambda\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\|_{\rm 2}\leq\delta\|\Lambda\|_{\rm 2}

for all nonnegative-definite matrices
\Lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, and

\|\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}\|_{\rm 2}\leq\sqrt{2}\delta\|\boldsymbol{Q}\|_{\rm 2}

for all
r\times r
matrices
\boldsymbol{Q}
.

Lemma G.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
be a matrix with
\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{\rm F}=1
and

{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}
be unit vectors respectively in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
.
Let
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}
be the top left singular vector of
\boldsymbol{M}
.
Then,

\big(\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\|_{\rm 2}^{2}\big)\wedge(1/2)\leq\|\vect(\boldsymbol{M})\vect(\boldsymbol{M})^{\top}-\vect(\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}^{\top})\vect(\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}^{\top})^{\top}\|_{\rm 2}^{2}.

(81)

Lemmas
G.1
and
G.2
are Propositions 5 and 3 in
Han and Zhang, 2023
, respectively.

Lemma G.3
.

Let
d,d_{j},d_{*},r\leq d\wedge d_{j}
be positive integers,
\epsilon>0
and

N_{d,\epsilon}=\lfloor(1+2/\epsilon)^{d}\rfloor
.

(i) For any norm
\|\cdot\|
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exist

M_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|M_{j}\|\leq 1
,
j=1,\ldots,N_{d,\epsilon}
,
such that

\displaystyle\max_{\|M\|\leq 1}\min_{1\leq j\leq N_{d,\epsilon}}\|M-M_{j}\|\leq\epsilon.

Consequently, for any linear mapping
f
and norm
\|\cdot\|_{*}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{M\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\|M\|\leq 1}\|f(M)\|_{*}\leq 2\max_{1\leq j\leq N_{d,1/2}}\|f(M_{j})\|_{*}.

(ii) Given
\epsilon>0
, there exist
U_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}

and
V_{j^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}\times r}
with
\|U_{j}\|_{2}\vee\|V_{j^{\prime}}\|_{2}\leq 1
such that

\displaystyle\max_{M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d^{\prime}},\|M\|_{2}\leq 1,\text{rank}(M)\leq r}\ \min_{j\leq N_{dr,\epsilon/2},j^{\prime}\leq N_{d^{\prime}r,\epsilon/2}}\|M-U_{j}V_{j^{\prime}}^{\top}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon.

Consequently, for any linear mapping
f
and norm
\|\cdot\|_{*}
in the range of
f
,

\sup_{M,\widetilde{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d^{\prime}},\|M-\widetilde{M}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon\atop{\|M\|_{2}\vee\|\widetilde{M}\|_{2}\leq 1\atop\text{rank}(M)\vee\text{rank}(\widetilde{M})\leq r}}\frac{\|f(M-\widetilde{M})\|_{*}}{\epsilon 2^{I_{r<d\wedge d^{\prime}}}}\leq\sup_{\|M\|_{2}\leq 1\atop\text{rank}(M)\leq r}\|f(M)\|_{*}\leq 2\max_{1\leq j\leq N_{dr,1/8}\atop 1\leq j^{\prime}\leq N_{d^{\prime}r,1/8}}\|f(U_{j}V_{j^{\prime}}^{\top})\|_{*}.

(82)

(iii) Given
\epsilon>0
, there exist
U_{j,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times r_{k}}

and
V_{j^{\prime},k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}_{k}\times r_{k}}
with
\|U_{j,k}\|_{2}\vee\|V_{j^{\prime},k}\|_{2}\leq 1
such that

\displaystyle\max_{M_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{k}^{\prime}},\|M_{k}\|_{2}\leq 1\atop\text{rank}(M_{k})\leq r_{k},\forall k\leq K}\ \min_{j_{k}\leq N_{d_{k}r_{k},\epsilon/2}\atop j^{\prime}_{k}\leq N_{d_{k}^{\prime}r_{k},\epsilon/2},\forall k\leq K}\Big\|\otimes_{k=2}^{K}M_{k}-\otimes_{k=2}^{K}(U_{j_{k},k}V_{j_{k}^{\prime},k}^{\top})\Big\|_{2}\leq\epsilon(K-1).

For any linear mapping
f
and norm
\|\cdot\|_{*}
in the range of
f
,

\sup_{M_{k},\widetilde{M}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{k}^{\prime}},\|M_{k}-\widetilde{M}_{k}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon\atop{\text{rank}(M_{k})\vee\text{rank}(\widetilde{M}_{k})\leq r_{k}\atop\|M_{k}\|_{2}\vee\|\widetilde{M}_{k}\|_{2}\leq 1\ \forall k\leq K}}\frac{\|f(\otimes_{k=2}^{K}M_{k}-\otimes_{k=2}^{K}\widetilde{M}_{k})\|_{*}}{\epsilon(2K-2)}\leq\sup_{M_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{k}^{\prime}}\atop{\text{rank}(M_{k})\leq r_{k}\atop\|M_{k}\|_{2}\leq 1,\forall k}}\Big\|f\big(\otimes_{k=2}^{K}M_{k}\big)\Big\|_{*}

(83)

and

\sup_{M_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{k}^{\prime}},\|M_{k}\|_{2}\leq 1\atop\text{rank}(M_{k})\leq r_{k}\ \forall k\leq K}\Big\|f\big(\otimes_{k=2}^{K}M_{k}\big)\Big\|_{*}\leq 2\max_{1\leq j_{k}\leq N_{d_{k}r_{k},1/(8K-8)}\atop 1\leq j_{k}^{\prime}\leq N_{d_{k}^{\prime}r_{k},1/(8K-8)}}\Big\|f\big(\otimes_{k=2}^{K}U_{j_{k},k}V_{j_{k}^{\prime},k}^{\top}\big)\Big\|_{*}.

(84)

Lemma G.4
.

Let
r\leq d_{1}\wedge d_{2}
,
M
be a
d_{1}\times d_{2}
matrix,

U
and
V
be, respectively, the left and right singular matrices associated
with the
r
largest singular values of
M
,

U_{\perp}
and
V_{\perp}
be the orthonormal complements of
U
and
V
,
and
\lambda_{r}
be the
r
-th largest singular value of
M
.
Let
\widehat{M}=M+\Delta
be a noisy version of
M
,

\{\widehat{U},\widehat{V},{\widehat{U}}_{\perp},{\widehat{V}}_{\perp}\}

be the counterpart of
\{U,V,V_{\perp},V_{\perp}\}
, and

{\widehat{\lambda}}_{r+1}
be the
(r+1)
-th largest singular value of
\widehat{M}
.
Let
\|\cdot\|
be a
matrix norm satisfying
\|ABC\|\leq\|A\|_{2}\|C\|_{2}\|B\|
,

\epsilon_{1}=\|U^{\top}\Delta{\widehat{V}}_{\perp}\|
and
\epsilon_{2}=\|{\widehat{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\Delta V\|
. Then,

\displaystyle\|U_{\perp}^{\top}\widehat{U}\|\leq\frac{{\widehat{\lambda}}_{r+1}\epsilon_{1}+\lambda_{r}\epsilon_{2}}{\lambda_{r}^{2}-{\widehat{\lambda}}_{r+1}^{2}}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{1}\vee\epsilon_{2}}{\lambda_{r}-{\widehat{\lambda}}_{r+1}}.

(85)

In particular, for the spectral norm
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{2}
,
\hbox{\rm error}_{1}=\|\Delta\|_{2}/\lambda_{r}

and
\hbox{\rm error}_{2}=\epsilon_{2}/\lambda_{r}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}\widehat{U}^{\top}-UU^{\top}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\hbox{\rm error}_{1}^{2}+\hbox{\rm error}_{2}}{1-\hbox{\rm error}_{1}^{2}}.

(86)

Lemma
G.3
applies an
\epsilon
-net argument for matrices, as derived in Lemma G.1 in
Han et al., 2024a
. Lemma
G.4
enhances the matrix perturbation bounds of
Wedin, 1972
, as derived in Lemma 4.1 in
Han et al., 2024a
. This sharper perturbation bound, detailed in the middle of (
85
), improves the commonly used version of the
Wedin, 1972
bound on the right-hand side,
compared with Theorem 1 of
Cai and Zhang, 2018
and Lemma 1 of
Chen et al., 2022
. As
Cai and Zhang, 2018
pointed out, such variations of the
Wedin, 1972
bound offer more precise convergence rates when when
\hbox{\rm error}_{2}\leq\hbox{\rm error}_{1}
in (
86
), typically in the case of
d_{1}\ll d_{2}
.

The following lemma characterizes the accuracy of estimating the inverse of the covariance matrix
\Sigma_{m}^{-1},m=1,\dots,M
using sample covariance, and the convergence rate of the normalization constant.

Lemma G.5
.

(i) Let
\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{-m}}
be i.i.d. random matrices, each following the matrix normal distribution
\boldsymbol{X}\sim{\cal M}{\cal N}_{d_{m}\times d_{-m}}(\mu,\;\Sigma_{m},\;\Sigma_{-m})
. To estimate
\Sigma_{m}
and its inverse
\Sigma_{m}^{-1}
, we utilize the sample covariance
\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}:=(nd_{-m})^{-1}\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\overline{\boldsymbol{X}})(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\overline{\boldsymbol{X}})^{\top}
with
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
, and its inverse
\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}
. Then there exists constants
C>0
such that if
nd_{-m}\gtrsim d_{m}(1\vee\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}^{2})
, in an event with probability at least
1-\exp(-cd_{m})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}\right\|_{2}\leq C\cdot\left\|\Sigma_{m}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}},

(87)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}-(C_{m,\sigma})^{-1}\Sigma_{m}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\leq C(C_{m,\sigma}^{-2}\vee C_{m,\sigma}^{-4})\left\|\Sigma_{m}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}},

(88)

where
C_{m,\sigma}=(1-n^{-1})\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{-m})/d_{-m}
.

(ii) Let
{\cal X}_{1},\cdots,{\cal X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
be i.i.d. random tensors, each following the tensor normal distribution
{\cal X}\sim{\cal T}{\cal N}(\mu,\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
, where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
and
\Sigma_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{m}}
. Define
\widehat{\Var}({\cal X}_{1\cdots 1})=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}({\cal X}_{i,1\cdots 1}-\overline{{\cal X}}_{1\cdots 1})^{2}
as the sample variance of the first element of
{\cal X}
, and
\Var({\cal X}_{1\cdots 1})=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m,11}
,
\overline{{\cal X}}_{1\cdots 1}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\cal X}_{i,1\cdots 1}
. Then in an event with probability at least
1-\exp(-c(t_{1}+t_{2}))
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\prod_{m=1}^{M}\widehat{\Sigma}_{m,11}}{\widehat{\Var}({\cal X}_{1\cdots 1})}-C_{\sigma}\right|\leq C_{M}\Var({\cal X}_{1\cdots 1})\left(\max_{m}\frac{\|\otimes_{k\neq m}\Sigma_{k}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d_{-m}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{t_{1}}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{t_{2}}{n}}\right),

(89)

where
C_{\sigma}=\prod_{m=1}^{M}C_{m,\sigma}=(1-n^{-1})^{M}[\prod_{m=1}^{M}\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{m})/d]^{M-1}=(1-n^{-1})^{M}[\mbox{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/d]^{M-1}
and
C_{M}
depends on
M
.

Proof.

We first show (
87
).
Note that
\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)^{\top}]=\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{-m})\cdot\Sigma_{m}=(n/(n-1))C_{m,\sigma}d_{-m}\cdot\Sigma_{m}
, and thus
\Sigma_{m}=\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)^{\top}](n-1)/(nC_{m,\sigma}d_{-m})
.
Consider a sequence of independent copies
\boldsymbol{Z}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{Z}_{n}
of
\boldsymbol{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times d_{-m}}
with entries
z_{ij}
that are i.i.d. and follow
N(0,1)
. The Gaussian random matrices
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
are then given by
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mu=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}
, where
\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}=\Sigma_{m}
and
\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}=\Sigma_{-m}
. Then,
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{nd_{-m}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mu)(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mu)^{\top}-\frac{\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{-m})}{d_{-m}}\cdot\Sigma_{m}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\left[\frac{1}{d_{-m}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}-\mu)(\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}-\mu)^{\top}-\frac{\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{-m})}{nd_{-m}}\cdot\Sigma_{m}\right].

For any unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}}
,

v^{\top}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mu)=v^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}=\vec 1(v^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top})=(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes v^{\top}\boldsymbol{A})\vec 1(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{\top}\left((\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mu)(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mu)^{\top}-\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)^{\top}]\right)v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{A}\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top})\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}\right)v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\vec 1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})(\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}v)(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes v^{\top}\boldsymbol{A})\vec 1(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})-\mbox{tr}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}\otimes\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}vv^{\top}\boldsymbol{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\vec 1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})(\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}v)(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes v^{\top}\boldsymbol{A})\vec 1(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})-\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{-m})v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v.

By Hanson-Wright inequality, for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[{\vec 1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})(\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}v)(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes v^{\top}\boldsymbol{A})\vec 1(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})-\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{-m})v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v\right]\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{16n\left\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}\otimes\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}vv^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}},\frac{t}{4\left\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}\otimes\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}vv^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\right\|_{2}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{16n\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v)^{2}},\frac{t}{4\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{2}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v)}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{16nd_{-m}\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{2}^{2}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v)^{2}},\frac{t}{4\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{2}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v)}\right\}\right).

Similarly, by Hanson-Wright inequality, for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(v^{\top}\left(n^{2}(\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}-\mu)(\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}-\mu)^{\top}-n\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)(\boldsymbol{X}-\mu)^{\top}]\right)v\geq t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left[\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}{\vec 1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})(\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}v)(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes v^{\top}\boldsymbol{A})\vec 1(\boldsymbol{Z}_{j})-n\mbox{tr}(\Sigma_{-m})v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v\right]\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\exp\left(-c\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{16n^{2}d_{-m}\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{2}^{2}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v)^{2}},\frac{t}{4n\left\|\Sigma_{-m}\right\|_{2}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{m}v)}\right\}\right).

By
\varepsilon-net
argument, there exist unit vectors
v_{1},...,v_{5^{p}}
such that for all
p\times p
symmetric matrix
M
,

\displaystyle\left\|M\right\|_{2}\leq 4\max_{j\leq 5^{p}}\left|v_{j}^{\top}Mv_{j}\right|.

(90)

See also Lemma 3 in
Cai et al., 2010
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}\right\|_{2}\geq x)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(4\max_{j\leq 5^{d_{m}}}\left|v_{j}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}\right)v_{j}\right|\geq x\right)

\displaystyle\leq 5^{d_{m}}\mathbb{P}\left(4\left|v_{j}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}\right)v_{j}\right|\geq x\right).

As
d_{m}\lesssim nd_{-m}
, this implies that with
x\asymp\|\Sigma_{m}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{d_{m}/(nd_{-m})}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}\right\|_{2}\geq C\|\Sigma_{m}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}}\right)\leq 5^{d_{m}}\exp\left(-c_{0}d_{m}\right)\leq\exp\left(-cd_{m}\right).

Second, we prove (
88
). For simplicity, denote
\Delta_{m}:=\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}=\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}
with
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}=C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m}
. Then write

\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{d_{m}}+\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\right)^{-1}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}.

Using Neumann series expansion, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\right)^{k}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}+\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\right)^{k}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}

Rearranging the term, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}=-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}+\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\right)^{k}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}

Employing similar arguments in the proof of (
87
), we can show that in an event
\Omega
with probability at least
1-\exp(-cd_{m})
,

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\leq CC_{m,\sigma}^{-1}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}}.

As
d_{m}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim nd_{-m}
, in the same event
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq CC_{m,\sigma}^{-2}\|\Sigma_{m}^{-1}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}},

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq CC_{m,\sigma}^{-3/2}\left\lVert\Sigma_{-m}\right\rVert\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}},

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\Delta_{m}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq CC_{m,\sigma}^{-1}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}C_{m,\sigma}^{-1}.

Combining the above bounds together, we have, in the event
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}-\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m}^{-1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq CC_{m,\sigma}^{-2}\|\Sigma_{m}^{-1}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}}+CC_{m,\sigma}^{-3}\left(\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}}\right)^{2}\cdot C_{m,\sigma}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq C(C_{m,\sigma}^{-2}\vee C_{m,\sigma}^{-4})\|\Sigma_{m}^{-1}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-m}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{m}}{nd_{-m}}}.

Next, we prove (
89
). Employing similar arguments in the proof of (
87
), we can show

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\widehat{\Sigma}_{m,11}-C_{m,\sigma}\Sigma_{m,11}|\geq C\Sigma_{m,11}\|\otimes_{k\neq m}\Sigma_{k}\|_{2}\sqrt{\frac{t_{1}}{nd_{-m}}}\right)\leq\exp(-c_{1}t_{1}).

Using tail probability bounds for
\chi_{n}^{2}
(see e.g. Lemma D.2 in
Ma, 2013
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\widehat{\rm Var}({\cal X}_{1\cdots 1})-\prod_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m,11}\right|\geq C\prod_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m,11}\sqrt{\frac{t_{2}}{n}}\right)\leq\exp(-c_{1}t_{2}).

It follows that in an event with probability at least
1-\exp(-c(t_{1}+t_{2}))
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\prod_{m=1}^{M}\widehat{\Sigma}_{m,11}}{\widehat{\Var}({\cal X}_{1\cdots 1})}-C_{\sigma}\right|\leq C_{M}\prod_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m,11}\left(\max_{m}\frac{\|\otimes_{k\neq m}\Sigma_{k}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{d_{-m}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{t_{1}}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{t_{2}}{n}}\right)

where
C_{M}
depends on
M
. As
\Var({\cal X}_{1\cdots 1})=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\Sigma_{m,11}
, we finish the proof of (
89
).

∎

The following lemma presents the tail bound for the spectral norm of a Gaussian random matrix.

Lemma G.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{E}
be an
p_{1}\times p_{2}
random matrix with
\boldsymbol{E}\sim{\cal M}{\cal N}_{p_{1}\times p_{2}}(0,\;\Sigma_{1},\;\Sigma_{2}).
Then for any
t>0
, with constant
C>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{2}\geq C\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}^{1/2}(\sqrt{p_{1}}+\sqrt{p_{2}}+t)\right)\leq\exp(-t^{2})

(91)

Let
\mathbb{O}_{p_{1},r}=\{\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times r},\;\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\}
be the set of all
p_{1}\times r
orthonormal columns and let
\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}
be the orthogonal complement of
\boldsymbol{U}
. Denote
\boldsymbol{E}_{12}=\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{V}_{\perp},\;\boldsymbol{E}_{21}=\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}\boldsymbol{V}
, where
\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathbb{O}_{p_{1}\times r},\;\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{O}_{p_{2}\times r}.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{E}_{21}\|_{2}\geq C\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}^{1/2}(\sqrt{p_{1}}+t)\right)\leq\exp(-t^{2})

(92)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{E}_{12}\|_{2}\geq C\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}^{1/2}(\sqrt{p_{2}}+t)\right)\leq\exp(-t^{2})

(93)

Proof.

Similar to (
90
), using
\varepsilon
-net argument for unit ball (see e.g. Lemma 5 in
Cai and Zhang, 2018
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{2}\geq 3u\right)\leq 7^{p_{1}+p_{2}}\cdot\max_{\|x\|_{2}\leq 1,\|y\|_{2}\leq 1}\mathbb{P}\left(|x^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}y|\geq u\right).

Decompose
\boldsymbol{E}=\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{Z}\Sigma_{2}^{1/2}
, where
\boldsymbol{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}},\;\boldsymbol{Z}_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d}}}{{\sim}}N(0,1).

Then,

\displaystyle x^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}y=\left(y^{\top}\Sigma_{2}^{1/2}\otimes x^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\right)\vec 1(\boldsymbol{Z})\sim N\left(0,y^{\top}\Sigma_{2}y\cdot x^{\top}\Sigma_{1}x\right).

By Chernoff bound of Gaussian random variables,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|x^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}y|\geq u\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{y^{\top}\Sigma_{2}y\cdot x^{\top}\Sigma_{1}x}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}}\right).

Setting
u\asymp\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}^{1/2}(\sqrt{p_{1}}+\sqrt{p_{2}}+t)
, for certain
C>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{2}\geq C\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}^{1/2}(\sqrt{p_{1}}+\sqrt{p_{2}}+t)\right)\leq 2\cdot 7^{p_{1}+p_{2}}\exp(-c(p_{1}+p_{2})-t^{2})\leq\exp(-t^{2}).

For
\boldsymbol{E}_{21}
, following the same
\epsilon
-net arguments, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{E}_{21}\|_{2}\geq 3u\right)\leq 7^{(p_{1}-r)+r}\cdot\max_{\|x\|_{2}\leq 1,\|y\|_{2}\leq 1}\mathbb{P}\left(|x^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}_{21}y|\geq u\right).

Note that

\displaystyle x^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}_{21}y

\displaystyle=x^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{Z}\Sigma_{2}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{V}y=\left(y^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}\Sigma_{2}^{1/2}\otimes x^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}\right)\vec 1(\boldsymbol{Z})

\displaystyle\sim N\left(0,y^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}\Sigma_{2}\boldsymbol{V}y\cdot x^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\Sigma_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{\perp}x\right).

By Chernoff bound of Gaussian random variables, setting
u\asymp\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}^{1/2}(\sqrt{p_{1}}+t)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{E}_{21}\|\geq C\|\Sigma_{1}\|_{2}^{1/2}\|\Sigma_{2}\|_{2}^{1/2}(\sqrt{p_{1}}+t)\right)\leq 2\cdot 7^{p_{1}}\exp(-c(p_{1})-t^{2})\leq\exp(-t^{2}).

Similarly, we can derive the tail bound of
\|\boldsymbol{E}_{12}\|_{2}
.
∎

The following lemma characterizes the maximum of norms for zero-mean Gaussian tensors after any projections.

Lemma G.7
.

Let
{\cal E}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
be a Gaussian tensor,
{\cal E}\sim{\cal T}{\cal N}(0;\;\frac{1}{n}\Sigma_{1}^{-1},\Sigma_{2}^{-1},\Sigma_{3}^{-1})
, where there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that
C_{0}^{-1}\leq\mathop{\min}\limits_{m\in\{1,2,3\}}\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{m})\leq\mathop{\max}\limits_{m\in\{1,2,3\}}\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{m})\leq C_{0}
. Then we have the following bound for projections, with probability at most
C\exp(-Ct(d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3}))
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\max}\limits_{\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times r_{2}},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}\times r_{3}}\atop\|\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\|_{2}\leq 1,\|\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\|_{2}\leq 1}\left\|\mat 1({\cal E}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top})\right\|_{2}\geq C\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\frac{\sqrt{d_{1}+r_{2}r_{3}}+\sqrt{1+t}(\sqrt{d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3}})}{\sqrt{n}},

(94)

for any t>0. Similar results also hold for
{\rm mat}_{2}({\cal E}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top})
and
{\rm mat}_{3}({\cal E}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top})
.

Meanwhile, we have with probability at most
\exp(-Ct(d_{1}r_{1}+d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3}))

\displaystyle\mathop{\max}\limits_{\boldsymbol{V}_{1},\boldsymbol{V}_{2},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times r_{m}}\atop\|\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\|_{2}\leq 1,\;m=1,2,3}\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\geq C\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{3}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot\frac{r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+(1+t)(d_{1}r_{1}+d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3})}{n},

(95)

for any
t>0.

Proof.

The key idea for the proof of this lemma is via
\epsilon
-net. We first prove (
94
). By Lemma
G.3
, for
m=1,2,3
, there exists
\epsilon
-nets:
\boldsymbol{V}_{m}^{(1)},\dots,\boldsymbol{V}_{m}^{(N_{m})}
for
\{\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times r_{m}}:\|\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\|_{2}\leq 1\}
,
|{\cal N}_{m}|\leq((4+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)^{d_{m}r_{m}}
, such that for any
\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times r_{m}}
satisfying
\|\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\|_{2}\leq 1
, there exists
\boldsymbol{V}_{m}^{(j)}
such that

\|\boldsymbol{V}_{m}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon.

For fixed
\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(i)}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(j)}
, we define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}^{(ij)}=\mat 1\left({\cal E}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(i)})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(j)})^{\top}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times(r_{2}r_{3})}.

It is easy to obtain that

\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}^{(ij)}\sim{\cal M}{\cal N}_{d_{1}\times r_{2}r_{3}}\left(0;\;\frac{1}{n}\Sigma_{1}^{-1},\;(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(i)}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(j)})\cdot\Sigma_{-1}^{-1}\cdot(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(i)}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(j)})^{\top}\right).
Then employing similar arguments of Lemma
G.6
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}^{(ij)}\|_{2}\leq C\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_{1}}+\sqrt{r_{2}r_{3}}+t}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-2\exp(-t^{2}).

Then we further have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\max\limits_{i,j}\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{1}^{(ij)}\|_{2}\leq C\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{-1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_{1}}+\sqrt{r_{2}r_{3}}+t}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-2((4+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)^{d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3}}\exp(-t^{2})

(96)

for all
t>0
. Denote

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm argmax}\limits_{\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times r_{2}},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}\times r_{3}}\atop\|\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\|_{2}\leq 1,\|\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\|_{2}\leq 1}\left\|\mat 1\left({\cal E}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\mathop{\max}\limits_{\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times r_{2}},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}\times r_{3}}\atop\|\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\|_{2}\leq 1,\|\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\|_{2}\leq 1}\left\|\mat 1\left({\cal E}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2}

Using
\varepsilon
-net arguments, we can find
1\leq i\leq N_{2}
and
1\leq j\leq N_{3}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon
and
\|\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon
. In this case, under (
96
),

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\left\|\mat 1\left({\cal E}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*})^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\mat 1\left({\cal E}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(i)})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(j)})^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mat 1\left({\cal E}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*}-\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(i)})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(j)})^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|\mat 1\left({\cal E}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*}-\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(j)})^{\top}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{-1}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_{1}}+\sqrt{r_{2}r_{3}}+t}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+\varepsilon M+\varepsilon M,

Therefore, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(M\leq C\cdot\frac{1}{1-2\varepsilon}\left\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma_{-1}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_{1}}+\sqrt{r_{2}r_{3}}+t}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\geq 1-2((4+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)^{d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3}}\exp(-t^{2})

By setting
\varepsilon=1/3
, and
t^{2}=2\log(13)(d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3})(1+x)
, we have proved the first part of the lemma.

Then we prove the claim (
95
). Consider a Gaussian tensor
{\cal Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}}
with entries
z_{ijk}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d}}}{{\sim}}N(0,\frac{1}{n}),
then we have
{\cal E}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}:={\cal Z}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2})\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2})\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}).

By Lemma 8 in
Zhang and Xia, 2018
, we know

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\left\|{\cal Z}\times_{1}(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{1})^{\top}\times_{2}(\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2})^{\top}\times_{3}(\Sigma_{3}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{3})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\left\|\left(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{3}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\right)\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{2}{n}\sqrt{t\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\right)\otimes\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\right)\otimes\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\Sigma_{3}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\right)\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}}+\frac{2t}{n}\left\|\left(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{3}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-t).

Note that for any given
\boldsymbol{V}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times r_{k}}
satisfying
\|\boldsymbol{V}_{k}\|_{2}\leq 1,k=1,2,3,
we have

\left\|\left(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{3}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\Sigma_{3}^{-1/2}\|_{2}:=C_{\lambda}^{1/2}.

Then,

\left\|\left(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\right)\otimes\left(\Sigma_{3}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\right)\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq C_{\lambda}r_{1}r_{2}r_{3},

and

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}\right)\otimes\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}\right)\otimes\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\Sigma_{3}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}\right)\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq C_{\lambda}^{2}r_{1}r_{2}r_{3},

we have for any fixed
\boldsymbol{V}_{1},\boldsymbol{V}_{2},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}
and
t>0
that

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|{\cal Z}\times_{1}(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{1})^{\top}\times_{2}(\Sigma_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2})^{\top}\times_{3}(\Sigma_{3}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{V}_{3})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\geq\frac{C_{\lambda}r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+2C_{\lambda}\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}t}+2C_{\lambda}t}{n}\right)\leq\exp(-t).

By geometric inequality,
2\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}t}\leq r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+t
, then we further have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\geq\frac{C_{\lambda}(2r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+3t)}{n}\right)\leq\exp(-t).

The rest proof for this claim is similar to the first part. One can find three
\varepsilon
-nets:
\boldsymbol{V}_{m}^{(1)},\dots,\boldsymbol{V}_{m}^{(N_{m})}
for
\{\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times r_{m}}:\|\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\|_{2}\leq 1\}
,
N_{m}\leq((4+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)^{d_{m}r_{m}}
, and we have the tail bound:

\displaystyle\max\limits_{\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(k)}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{(i)})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(k)})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\geq\frac{C_{\lambda}(2r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+3t)}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-t)\cdot((4+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)^{d_{1}r_{1}+d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3}},

(97)

for all
t>0
. Assume

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{*},\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm argmax}\limits_{\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{m}\times r_{m}}\atop\|\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\|_{2}\leq 1}\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{\top}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{\top}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{*})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}

Then we can find
\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(k)}
in the corresponding
\varepsilon
-nets such that
\|\boldsymbol{V}_{m}^{*}-\boldsymbol{V}_{m}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon
,
m=1,2,3
. And

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{*})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{(i)})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(k)})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{*})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{(i)})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{(i)})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\lambda}(2r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+3t)}{n}+3\varepsilon T

which implies

\displaystyle\left\|{\cal E}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{*})^{\top}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*})^{\top}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{V}_{3}^{*})^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{\lambda}\cdot(2r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+3t)}{n(1-3\varepsilon)}.

If we set
\varepsilon=1/9
and
t=(1+x)\log(37)\cdot(d_{1}r_{1}+d_{2}r_{2}+d_{3}r_{3})
, by (
G
) we have proved (
95
).
∎

Lemma G.8
.

Suppose that
{\cal X}_{1},...,{\cal X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}
are i.i.d.
{\cal T}{\cal N}(\mu,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=[\Sigma_{m}]_{m=1}^{M}
, and
\overline{{\cal X}}
is the sample mean. Then, with probability at least
1-e^{-c\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}r}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\cal V}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}w_{i}\circ_{m=1}^{M}u_{im}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{M}}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{r}w_{i}^{2}\leq 1,\|u_{im}\|_{2}=1,\forall i\leq r,m\leq M\end{subarray}}\left|<\overline{{\cal X}}-\mu,{\cal V}>\right|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_{m}r}{n}}.

(98)

Proof.

For any CP low-rank tensor
{\cal V}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}w_{i}\circ_{m=1}^{M}u_{im}
with
w_{i}>0
and
\|u_{im}\|_{2}=1
, we can reformulate a Tucker type decomposition,
